The **Temple Mount** or **al-Haram al-Sharif** ([Biblical Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew_language): הַר הַבַּיִת, romanized: *Har hab-Bayiṯ*, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الحَرَم الشَّرِيف, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *al-Ḥaram aš-Šarīf* or الأَقْصَى, *al-Aqṣā*) is an elevated plateau in the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md) that comprises the Islamic religious compound known as [al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa), which includes the [al-Aqsa Mosque](65759.md) and the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock). Historically the location of two successive [Jewish temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem), the area has been venerated as a [holy site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_space) for thousands of years including in [Judaism](15624.md), [Christianity](5211.md), and [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-Kedar_20122-2][^cite_note-Weaver_2018_p._7722-3]

The present site is a flat plaza surrounded by [retaining walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retaining_wall) (including the [Western Wall](75225.md)), which were originally built by [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great) in the first century BCE to expand the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple). The plaza is dominated by two monumental structures originally built during the Rashidun and early Umayyad caliphates after the 637 [first Muslim conquest of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Muslim_conquest_of_Jerusalem):[^cite_note-Nicolle,_David_19942-4] the Qibli Mosque of al-Aqsa and the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock), near the center of the hill, which was completed in 692, making it one of the oldest extant Muslim structures in the world. The [Herodian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_dynasty) walls and gates, with additions from the late [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), [early Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Jerusalem_during_the_Early_Muslim_period), [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate), and [Ottoman](22278.md) periods, flank the site, which can be reached through [eleven gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount), ten reserved for Muslims and one for non-Muslims, with guard posts of the [Israel Police](745595.md) in the vicinity of each.[^cite_note-5] The courtyard is surrounded on the north and west by two [Mamluk-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_architecture) porticos or arcades (*[arwiqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riwaq_\(arcade\))*) and [four minarets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minarets_of_Al-Aqsa).

The Temple Mount is the holiest site in Judaism,[^cite_note-\:112-6][^cite_note-\:110-7][^cite_note-9] and where the Temples in Jerusalem once stood.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] According to [Jewish](15624.md) and [Samaritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritanism) tradition and scriptures, the first Temple was [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple), built by King [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon), the son of King [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David), in 957 BCE, and was destroyed along with the city itself by the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) in the [Siege of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(587_BC\)), in 587 BCE. No archaeological evidence has been found to verify the existence of the First Temple, and scientific excavations are limited due to religious sensitivities.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-PullanSternberg20132-14][^cite_note-15]

The Second Temple, constructed under [Zerubbabel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zerubbabel) in 516 BCE, was later [renovated by King Herod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod's_Temple) and [destroyed by the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)) in 70 CE. [Judaism](15624.md) maintains it is here that the [third and final Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Temple) will be built when the [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism) comes.[^cite_note-\:122-16]

Jews face the Temple Mount [during prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_prayer). Jewish attitudes towards entering the site vary. Due to its extreme sanctity, many Jews will not walk on the Mount itself to avoid unintentionally entering the area where the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies) stood, since, according to rabbinical law, there is still some aspect of the [divine presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shekhinah) at the site.[^cite_note-ReferenceA2-17][^cite_note-\:132-18][^cite_note-\:142-19]

In Islam, the Mount is believed to be the [second mosque mentioned by the *Quran*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_oldest_mosques)[^cite_note-NG-20] and one of the three Sacred Mosques, the [holiest sites in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam); it is revered as "the Noble Sanctuary".[^cite_note-\:22-21] Its courtyard (*[sahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahn)*)[^cite_note-Prawer-22] can host more than 400,000 worshippers, making it one of the [largest mosques in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_mosques).[^cite_note-NG-20]

For [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) and [Shia](26961.md) Muslims alike, it ranks as [the third holiest site in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Sunni_Islam). The plaza includes the location regarded as where [Muhammad ascended bodily into heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj),[^cite_note-23] and served as the initial [qibla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla), the direction Muslims turn towards when praying. As in Judaism, Muslims also associate the site with [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_in_Islam) and other prophets also venerated in Islam.[^cite_note-Quran_2\:4,_34\:13-14-24] The site, and the term "al-Aqsa", in relation to the whole plaza, is also a central identity symbol for [Palestinians](23267.md), including [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians).[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27]

Since the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades), the Muslim community of Jerusalem has managed the site through the [Jerusalem Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Waqf). The site, along with the whole of [East Jerusalem](343508.md) (which includes the Old City), was controlled by [Jordan](7515964.md) from 1948 until 1967 and has been [occupied by Israel](59076253.md) since the [Six-Day War](29328.md) of 1967. Shortly after capturing the site, Israel handed its administration back to the Waqf under the [Hashemite (Jordanian) custodianship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemite_custodianship_of_Jerusalem_holy_sites) while maintaining Israeli security control.[^cite_note-28] [Israel](9282173.md) enforces a ban on prayer by non-Muslims as part of an arrangement usually referred to as the "status quo".[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31] The site remains a major focal point of the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md).[^cite_note-32]

## Terminology

The name of the site is disputed, primarily between Muslims and Jews, in the context of the ongoing [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md). Some Arab-Muslim commentators and scholars [attempt to deny Jewish connection with the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_denial), while some Jewish commentators and scholars attempt to belittle the importance of the site in Islam.[^cite_note-\:18-33][^cite_note-34] During a 2016 dispute over the name of the site, [UNESCO](21786641.md) Director-General [Irina Bokova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irina_Bokova) stated: "Different peoples worship the same places, sometimes under different names. The recognition, use of and respect for these names is paramount."[^cite_note-35]

### Temple Mount

The term *Har haBayīt* – commonly translated as "Temple Mount" in English – was first used in the books of [Micah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Micah) (4\:1) and [Jeremiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Jeremiah) (26\:18), literally as "Mount of the House", a literary variation of the longer phrase "Mountain of the House of the Lord". The abbreviation was not used again in the later books of the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200850–51-36] or in the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200856-37] The term remained in use throughout the [Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period), although the term "Mount Zion", which today refers to the [eastern hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion) of ancient Jerusalem, was used more frequently. Both terms are in use in the [Book of Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_the_Maccabees).[^cite_note-\:20-38] The term *Har haBayīt* is used throughout the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) and later Talmudic texts.[^cite_note-Eliav_2003_pp._49–113-39][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200859b-40]

The exact moment when the concept of the Mount as a topographical feature separate from the Temple or the city itself first came into existence is a matter of debate among scholars.[^cite_note-\:20-38] According to Eliav, it was during the first century CE, after the destruction of the Second Temple.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200864-41] Shahar and Shatzman reached different conclusions.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] In the [Books of Chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_Chronicles), edited at the end of the [Persian period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_\(Persian_province\)), the mountain is already referred to as a distinct entity. In 2 Chronicles, [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) was constructed on Mount [Moriah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriah) (3\:1), and [Manasseh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasseh_of_Judah)'s atonement for his sins is associated with the Mountain of the House of the Lord (33\:15).[^cite_note-\:15-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-\:20-38] The conception of the Temple as being located on a holy mountain possessing special qualities is found repeatedly in Psalms, with the surrounding area being considered an integral part of the Temple itself.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200854-46]

The governmental organization which administers the site, the [Jerusalem Islamic Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Islamic_Waqf) (part of the Jordanian government), have stated that the name "The Temple Mount" is a "strange and alien name" and a "newly-created Judaization term".[^cite_note-47] In 2014, the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO) issued a press release urging journalists not to use the term "Temple Mount" when referring to the site.[^cite_note-48] In 2017, it was reported that Waqf officials harassed archeologists such as [Gabriel Barkay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Barkay) and tour guides who used the term at the site.[^cite_note-\:19-49] According to Jan Turek and John Carman, in modern usage, the term Temple Mount can potentially imply support for Israeli control of the site.[^cite_note-50]

### Other Hebrew terms

[2 Chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_Chronicles) 3\:1[^cite_note-\:15-44] refers to the Temple Mount in the time before the construction of the temple as Mount Moriah ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): הַר הַמֹּורִיָּה, *har ha-Môriyyāh*).

Several passages in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) indicate that during the time when they were written, the Temple Mount was identified as Mount Zion.[^cite_note-Pixner-51] The [Mount Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion) mentioned in the later parts of the [Book of Isaiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Isaiah) (Isaiah 60\:14),[^cite_note-52] in the [Book of Psalms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Psalms), and the [First Book of Maccabees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Book_of_Maccabees) (c. 2nd century BCE) seems to refer to the top of the hill, generally known as the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-Pixner-51] According to the [Book of Samuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Samuel), Mount Zion was the site of the Jebusite fortress called the "stronghold of Zion", but once the First Temple was erected, according to the Bible, at the top of the Eastern Hill ("Temple Mount"), the name "Mount Zion" migrated there too.[^cite_note-Pixner-51] The name later migrated for a last time, this time to Jerusalem's Western Hill.[^cite_note-Pixner-51]

### Al-Aqsa Mosque



Extract of an [1841 British map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1840%E2%80%9341_Royal_Engineers_maps_of_Palestine,_Lebanon_and_Syria) showing both "Mesjid el-Aksa" and "Jami el-Aksa"

The English term "al-Aqsa Mosque" is a translation of either *al-Masjid al-'Aqṣā* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): ٱلْمَسْجِد ٱلْأَقْصَىٰ) or *al-Jâmi' al-Aqṣā* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): ٱلْـجَـامِـع الْأَقْـصّى).[^cite_note-Robinson-53][^cite_note-Palmer-54][^cite_note-PEF-55] *Al-Masjid al-'Aqṣā* – "the farthest mosque" – is derived from the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran)'s *[Surah 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Isra)* ("The Night Journey") which writes that [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam) travelled from Mecca to the mosque, from where he subsequently ascended to [Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heaven).[^cite_note-auto-56][^cite_note-57] Arabic and Persian writers such as 10th-century geographer [Al-Maqdisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Maqdisi),[^cite_note-MukaddasiNasir-58] 11th-century scholar [Nasir Khusraw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasir_Khusraw),[^cite_note-MukaddasiNasir-58] 12th-century geographer [Muhammad al-Idrisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Idrisi)[^cite_note-59] and 15th-century Islamic scholar [Mujir al-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujir_al-Din),[^cite_note-MujiralDin-60][^cite_note-61] as well as 19th century American and British [Orientalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalism) [Edward Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Robinson_\(scholar\)),[^cite_note-Robinson-53] [Guy Le Strange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Le_Strange) and [Edward Henry Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Henry_Palmer) explained that the term *Masjid al-Aqsa* refers to the entire esplanade plaza which is the subject of this article – the entire area including the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock), the fountains, the [gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount), and the [four minarets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minarets_of_the_Temple_Mount) – because none of these buildings existed at the time the Quran was written.[^cite_note-Palmer-54][^cite_note-62][^cite_note-Strange_1887_pp._247–305-63]

*Al-Jâmi' al-Aqṣá* refers to the specific site of the silver-domed [congregational mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_mosque) building,[^cite_note-Robinson-53][^cite_note-Palmer-54][^cite_note-PEF-55] also referred to as [Qibli Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibli_Mosque) or Qibli Chapel (*al-Jami' al-Aqsa* or *al-Qibli*, or *Masjid al-Jumah* or *al-Mughata*), in reference to its location on the southern end of the compound as a result of the Islamic [qibla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla) being moved from Jerusalem to Mecca.[^cite_note-64] The two different Arabic terms, translated as "mosque" in English, parallel the two different Greek terms translated as "temple" in the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament): [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): ίερόν, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *hieron* (equivalent to *Masjid*) and [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): ναός, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *naos* (equivalent to *Jami'a*),[^cite_note-Robinson-53][^cite_note-MujiralDin-60][^cite_note-Carpenter_Comfort_2000_p._404-65] and use of the term "mosque" for the whole compound follows the usage of the same term for other early Islamic sites with large courtyards such as the [Mosque of Ibn Tulun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosque_of_Ibn_Tulun) in Cairo, the [Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque) in Damascus and the [Great Mosque of Kairouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Kairouan).[^cite_note-66] Other sources and maps have used the term *al-Masjid al-'Aqṣā* to refer to the congregational mosque itself.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]



Extract of a [1936 British map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survey_of_Palestine) showing the entire site as "Moriah" or "Haram esh-Sharif"; the Al-Aqsa Mosque shown as "Mesjid el-Aksa"

The term "al-Aqsa" as a symbol and brand-name has become popular and prevalent in the region.[^cite_note-Reiter2-70] For example, the [Al-Aqsa Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Intifada) (the uprising of September 2000), the [al-Aqsa Martyrs' Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Martyrs'_Brigades) (a coalition of Palestinian nationalist militias in the West Bank), [al-Aqsa TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_TV) (the official Hamas-run television channel), [al-Aqsa University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_University) (Palestinian university established in 1991 in the Gaza Strip), [Jund al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_al-Aqsa) (a Salafist jihadist organization that was active during the Syrian Civil War), the Jordanian military periodical published since the early 1970s, and the associations of both the southern and northern branches of the [Islamic Movement in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Movement_in_Israel) are all named Al-Aqsa after this site.[^cite_note-Reiter2-70]

### al-Haram ash-Sharif

During the period of [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate)[^cite_note-71] (1260–1517) and [Ottoman](22278.md) rule (1517–1917), the wider compound of the Temple Mount began to also be popularly known as the Haram al-Sharif, or الحَرَم الشَّرِيف, *al-ḥaram aš-šharīf*, which translates as the 'Noble Sanctuary,' or الحَرَم القُدْسِي الشَّرِيف, *al-ḥaram al-qudsī aš-šharīf*, the 'Noble Jerusalem/Holy Sanctuary.'[^cite_note-\:25-72] The [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) word *ḥaram* (حَرَم)—related to but distinct from the word *[ḥarām](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram)* (حَرام)—is used to refer to a [campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campus) or a [compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compound_\(enclosure\)), especially of a mosque, as in *ḥaram jāmiʿī* (حَرَم جَامِعيّ).[^cite_note-\:111-73]

It mirrors the terminology of the [Masjid al-Haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_al-Haram) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca);[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-\:25-72][^cite_note-76] This term elevated the compound to the status of [Haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram), which had previously been reserved for the Masjid al-Haram in Mecca, and the [Al-Masjid an-Nabawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Masjid_an-Nabawi) in [Medina](36636.md). Other Islamic figures disputed the haram status of the site.[^cite_note-Reiter2-70] Usage of the name *Haram al-Sharif* by local Palestinians has waned in recent decades, in favor of the traditional name of Al-Aqsa Mosque.[^cite_note-Reiter2-70]

### Jerusalem's sacred esplanade

Some scholars have used the terms Sacred [Esplanade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esplanade) or Holy Esplanade as a "strictly neutral term" for the site.[^cite_note-Kedar_20122-2][^cite_note-Weaver_2018_p._7722-3] A notable example of this usage is the 2009 work *[Where Heaven and Earth Meet: Jerusalem's Sacred Esplanade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Where_Heaven_and_Earth_Meet)*, written as a joint undertaking by 21 Jewish, Muslim and Christian scholars.[^cite_note-Ole09-77][^cite_note-Day12-78]

### Jerusalem's Holy Esplanade

In recent years, the term "Holy Esplanade" has been used by the [United Nations](31769.md), by its [Secretary-General](162415.md) and by the UN's subsidiary organs.[^cite_note-79]

## Location and dimensions



Topographical map of Jerusalem, showing the Temple Mount on the eastern peak

The Temple Mount forms the northern portion of a narrow spur of hill that slopes sharply downward from north to south. Rising above the [Kidron Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidron_Valley) to the east and [Tyropoeon Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyropoeon_Valley) to the west,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen20039–11-80] its peak reaches a height of 740 m (2,428 ft) above sea level.[^cite_note-Lundquist2007p.103-81] In around 19 BCE, [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great) extended the Mount's natural [plateau](538005.md) by enclosing the area with four massive retaining walls and filling the voids. This artificial expansion resulted in a large flat expanse which today forms the eastern section of the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md). The [trapezium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trapezoid) shaped platform measures 488 m (1,601 ft) along the west, 470 m (1,540 ft) along the east, 315 m (1,033 ft) along the north and 280 m (920 ft) along the south, giving a total area of approximately 150,000 m<sup>2</sup> (37 acres).[^cite_note-Finkelstein1999p43-82] The northern wall of the Mount, together with the northern section of the western wall, is hidden behind residential buildings. The southern section of the western flank is revealed and contains what is known as the [Western Wall](75225.md). The retaining walls on these two sides descend many meters below ground level. A northern portion of the western wall may be seen from within the [Western Wall Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Wall_Tunnel), which was excavated through buildings adjacent to the platform. On the southern and eastern sides, the walls are visible almost to their full height. The platform itself is separated from the rest of the Old City by the Tyropoeon Valley, though this once deep valley is now largely hidden beneath later deposits and is imperceptible in places. The platform can be reached via Gate of the Chain Street – a street in the [Muslim Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Quarter_\(Jerusalem\)) at the level of the platform, actually sitting on a monumental bridge;[^cite_note-83] the bridge is no longer externally visible due to the change in ground level, but it can be seen from beneath via the Western Wall Tunnel.[^cite_note-84]

## Heritage site

In 1980, [Jordan](7515964.md) proposed that the Old City be listed as a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md)[^cite_note-85] and it was added to the List in 1981.[^cite_note-86] In 1982, it was added to the [List of World Heritage in Danger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_in_Danger).[^cite_note-87]

On 26 October 2016, UNESCO passed the [Occupied Palestine Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupied_Palestine_Resolution) that condemned what it described as "escalating Israeli aggressions" and illegal measures against the waqf, called for the restoration of Muslim access and demanded that Israel respect the historical status quo[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-AJUNESCO-90] and also criticized Israel for its continuous "refusal to let the body's experts access Jerusalem's holy sites to determine their conservation status".[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] While the text acknowledged the "importance of the Old City of Jerusalem and its walls for the three monotheistic religions", it referred to the sacred hilltop compound in Jerusalem's Old City only by its Muslim name Al-Haram al-Sharif.

In response, Israel denounced the UNESCO resolution for its omission of the words "Temple Mount" or "Har HaBayit", stating that it denied Jewish ties to the site.[^cite_note-AJUNESCO-90][^cite_note-93] Israel froze all ties with UNESCO.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95] In October 2017, Israel and the United States announced they would withdraw from UNESCO, citing anti-Israel bias.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

On 6 April 2022, UNESCO unanimously adopted a resolution reiterating all 21 previous resolutions concerned with Jerusalem.[^cite_note-98]

## Religious significance

See also: [Religious significance of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_significance_of_Jerusalem)

The Temple Mount has historical and religious significance for all three of the major [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions): Judaism, Christianity and Islam. It has particular religious significance for Judaism and Islam.

### Judaism

See also: [Jerusalem in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Judaism)

The Temple Mount is considered the holiest site in Judaism.[^cite_note-\:112-6][^cite_note-\:1-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen20034-8] According to Jewish tradition, both [Temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) stood at the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-BBC_-_Science_&_Nature_-_Horizon-100] Jewish tradition further places the Temple Mount as the location for a number of important events which occurred in the Bible, including the [Binding of Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binding_of_Isaac), Jacob's dream, and the prayer of Isaac and [Rebekah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_\(biblical_figure\)).[^cite_note-101] According to the Talmud, the [Foundation Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundation_Stone) is the place from where the world was created and expanded into its current form.[^cite_note-\:16-102][^cite_note-\:17-103] [Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Jew) tradition maintains it is here that the [third and final Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Temple) will be built when the [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism) comes.[^cite_note-\:12-104]

The Temple Mount is the place Jews turn towards during prayer. Jewish attitudes towards entering the site vary. Due to its extreme sanctity, many Jews will not walk on the Mount itself, to avoid unintentionally entering the area where the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies) stood, since, according to rabbinical law, there is still some aspect of the [divine presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shekhinah) at the site.[^cite_note-ReferenceA2-17][^cite_note-\:13-105][^cite_note-\:142-19]

#### The Temple

See also: [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem)



The [Holyland Model of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holyland_Model_of_Jerusalem) depicts [Jerusalem during the late Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_during_the_Second_Temple_Period). The Temple Mount and Herod's Temple are shown in the middle. View from the east.

According to the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible), the Temple Mount was originally a [threshing-floor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threshing-floor) owned by [Araunah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Araunah), a [Jebusite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jebusite).[^cite_note-106] The Bible narrates how [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David) united the [twelve Israelite tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Tribes_of_Israel), conquered Jerusalem and brought the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites)' central artifact, the [Ark of the Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ark_of_the_Covenant), into the city.[^cite_note-107] When a great plague struck Israel, a [destroying angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destroying_angel_\(Bible\)) appeared on Araunah's threshing floor. The prophet [Gad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gad_\(Bible_prophet\)) then suggested the area to David as a fitting place for the erection of an altar to [Yahweh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahweh).[^cite_note-108] David bought the property from Araunah, for fifty pieces of silver, and erected the altar. God answered his prayers and stopped the plague. David subsequently chose the site for a future temple to replace the [Tabernacle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabernacle) and house the Ark of the Covenant;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Temple_of_Jerusalem''-109][^cite_note-eastons-110] [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) forbade him from building it, however, because he had "shed much blood".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonker1990656-111]

The [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) was instead constructed under David's son [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon),[^cite_note-112] who became an ambitious builder of public works in [ancient Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah):[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStefon2020-113]

> Then Solomon began to build the house of the LORD at Jerusalem in Mount Moriah, where \[the LORD] appeared unto David his father; for which provision had been made in the Place of David, in the threshing floor of Ornan the Jebusite.

— 2 Chronicles 3\:1[^cite_note-114]

Solomon placed the Ark in the Holy of Holies – the windowless innermost sanctuary and most sacred area of the temple in which God's presence rested;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Britannica:_Holy_of_Holies''-115] entry into the Holy of Holies was heavily restricted, and only the [High Priest of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Priest_of_Israel) entered the sanctuary once per year on [Yom Kippur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur), carrying the blood of a sacrificial lamb and burning [incense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incense_offering_in_rabbinic_literature).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Britannica:_Holy_of_Holies''-115] According to the Bible, the site functioned as the center of all national life – a governmental, judicial and religious center.[^cite_note-116]

The [Genesis Rabba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genesis_Rabba), which was probably written between 300 and 500 CE, states that this site is one of three about which the nations of the world cannot taunt Israel and say, "you have stolen them," since it was purchased "for its full price" by David.[^cite_note-117]

The First Temple [was destroyed in 587/586 BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(587_BC\)) by the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) under the second Babylonian king, [Nebuchadnezzar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebuchadnezzar_II), who subsequently [exiled the Judeans to Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_captivity) following the fall of the [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) and [its annexation as a Babylonian province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_\(Babylonian_province\)). The Jews who had been deported in the aftermath of the Babylonian conquest of Judah were eventually [allowed to return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Return_to_Zion) following [a proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Cyrus) by the Persian king [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) that was issued after the [fall of Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Babylon) to the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire). In 516 BCE, the returned Jewish population in Judah, under [Persian provincial governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_\(Persian_province\)), rebuilt the Temple in Jerusalem under the auspices of [Zerubbabel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zerubbabel), producing what is known as the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple).

During the [Second Temple Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period), Jerusalem was the center of religious and national life for Jews, including those in the [Diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora).[^cite_note-\:62-118] The Second Temple is believed to have attracted tens and maybe hundreds of thousands during the [Three Pilgrimage Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Pilgrimage_Festivals).[^cite_note-\:62-118] The holiday of [Hanukkah](7388.md) commemorates the rededication of the Temple at the beginning of the [Maccabean revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabean_revolt) in the 2nd century BCE. During the first century BCE, the Temple was renovated by [Herod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great). It [was destroyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)#Destruction_of_Jerusalem) by the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) at the height of the [First Jewish-Roman War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_War) in 70 CE. [Tisha B'Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_B'Av), an annual [fast day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta'anit) in [Judaism](15624.md), marks the destruction of the First and Second Temples, which according to Jewish tradition, occurred on the same day on the [Hebrew calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_calendar).

#### In prophecy

The [Book of Isaiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Isaiah) foretells the international importance of the Temple Mount:

> And it shall come to pass in the end of days, that the mountain of the LORD'S house shall be established as the top of the mountains, and shall be exalted above the hills; and all nations shall flow unto it. And many peoples shall go and say: 'Come ye, and let us go up to the mountain of the LORD, to the house of the God of Jacob; and He will teach us of His ways, and we will walk in His paths.' For out of [Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zion) shall go forth the law, and the word of the LORD from Jerusalem.

— Isaiah 2\:2–3[^cite_note-119]

#### Binding of Isaac

In Jewish tradition, the Temple Mount is also believed to be the location of Abraham's [binding of Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binding_of_Isaac). [2 Chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_Chronicles) 3\:1[^cite_note-\:15-44] refers to the Temple Mount in the time before the construction of the temple as **Mount Moriah** ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): הַר הַמֹּורִיָּה, *har ha-Môriyyā*). The "[land of Moriah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriah)" (אֶרֶץ הַמֹּרִיָּה, *ereṣ ha-Môriyyā*) is the name given by [Genesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Genesis) to the location of the binding of Isaac.[^cite_note-Delaney-120] Since at least the first century CE, the two sites have been identified with one another in Judaism, this identification being subsequently perpetuated by [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_legend) and [Christian tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_legend). Modern scholarship tends to regard them as distinct (see [Moriah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriah)).

#### Creation of the world



Picture showing what is presumed to be the [Foundation Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundation_Stone), or a large part of it

According to the rabbinic sages whose debates produced the [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud), the [Foundation Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundation_Stone), which sits below the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock), was the spot from where the world was created and expanded into its current form,[^cite_note-\:16-102][^cite_note-\:17-103] and where God gathered the dust used to create the first human, [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_\(Bible\)).[^cite_note-Delaney-120]

#### Third Temple

Jewish texts predict that the Mount will be the site of a [Third and final Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Temple), which will be rebuilt with the coming of the [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism). The rebuilding of the Temple remained a recurring theme among generations, particularly in thrice daily [Amidah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amidah) (Standing prayer), central prayer of the [Jewish liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_liturgy), which contains a plea for the building of a Third Temple and the restoration of [sacrificial services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korban).&#x20;

A few very small Jewish groups today support constructing a Third Temple,[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] but most Jews oppose this,[^cite_note-124] both due to the enormously hostile reaction from Palestinians and Arab nations that would likely result,[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] and because according to the Talmud and traditional halakha, the reconstruction of the Temple and accession to the sacred precinct carry severe ritual impurity restrictions, with many authorities arguing the rebuilding awaits the messianic era rather than human agency.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] Furthermore, reconstruction would require the reinstitution of ancient sacrificial rites (*korbanot*), a prospect that lacks widespread support across modern streams of Judaism.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] Some groups even advocate building the Third Temple without delay in order to bring to pass God's "end-time prophetic plans for Israel and the entire world."[^cite_note-133]

Some fundamentalist and evangelical Christian groups, particularly those adhering to [dispensational premillennialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dispensationalism), believe that the Jewish people will construct a Third Temple on the Temple Mount as an essential precursor to the [Great Tribulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Tribulation), occurring either shortly before or after the [rapture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapture) of the Christian church.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] This eschatological framework relies on interpretations of [Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Daniel) 9\:27, [Matthew 24\:15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_24), and [2 Thessalonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Epistle_to_the_Thessalonians) 2\:3–4, which dispensationalist authors interpret as requiring a physical temple in Jerusalem where the [Antichrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antichrist) will commit the "[abomination of desolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abomination_of_desolation)" before the [Second Coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) of Jesus.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] As a result, several American evangelical organizations actively provide financial and political support to Israeli Temple activist organizations, anticipating that rebuilding the sanctuary will accelerate the biblical end times.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

### Christianity

See also: [Jerusalem in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Christianity)

The Temple was of central importance in Jewish worship in the [Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh) ([Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament)). In the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), [Herod's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod's_Temple) was the site of several events in the life of [Jesus](1095706.md), and Christian loyalty to the site as a focal point remained long after his death.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] After the destruction of the Temple in 70 CE, which came to be regarded by early Christians, as it was by [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) and the sages of the [Jerusalem Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Talmud), to be a divine act of punishment for the sins of the Jewish people,[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] the Temple Mount lost its significance for Christian worship with the Christians considering it a fulfillment of Christ's prophecy at, for example, Matthew 23\:38[^cite_note-146] and Matthew 24\:2.[^cite_note-147] It was to this end, proof of a biblical prophecy fulfilled and of Christianity's [victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersessionism) over Judaism with the [New Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Covenant),[^cite_note-Marsham-148] that early Christian pilgrims also visited the site.[^cite_note-149] Byzantine Christians, despite some signs of constructive work on the esplanade,[^cite_note-150] generally neglected the Temple Mount, especially when a Jewish attempt to rebuild the Temple was destroyed by the [earthquake of 363](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galilee_earthquake_of_363).[^cite_note-Shick-151] It became a desolate local rubbish dump, perhaps outside the city limits,[^cite_note-152] as Christian worship in Jerusalem shifted to the [Church of the Holy Sepulchre](7810.md), and Jerusalem's centrality was replaced by Rome.[^cite_note-153]

During the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) era, Jerusalem was primarily Christian and pilgrims came by the tens of thousands to experience the places where Jesus walked. After the [Persian invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_conquest_and_occupation_of_Jerusalem) in 614 many churches were razed, and the site was turned into a dump. The Arabs [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(637\)) the city from the Byzantine Empire which had retaken it in 629. The Byzantine ban on the Jews was lifted and they were allowed to live inside the city and visit the places of worship. Christian pilgrims were able to come and experience the Temple Mount area.[^cite_note-154] The war between Seljuqs and Byzantine Empire and increasing Muslim violence against Christian pilgrims to Jerusalem instigated the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades). The Crusaders captured Jerusalem in 1099 and the Dome of the Rock was given to the [Augustinians](144980.md), who turned it into a church, and al-Aqsa Mosque became the royal palace of [Baldwin I of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_I_of_Jerusalem) in 1104. The [Knights Templar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Templar), who believed the Dome of the Rock was the site of [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Solomon), gave it the name "[Templum Domini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templum_Domini)" and set up their headquarters in al-Aqsa Mosque adjacent to the Dome for much of the 12th century.

In [Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art), the [circumcision of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcision_of_Jesus) was conventionally depicted as taking place at the Temple, even though European artists until recently had no way of knowing what the Temple looked like and the Gospels do not state that the event took place at the Temple.[^cite_note-155]

Though some Christians believe that the Temple will be reconstructed before, or concurrent with, the [Second Coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) of Jesus (also see [dispensationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dispensationalism)), pilgrimage to the Temple Mount is not viewed as important in the beliefs and worship of most Christians. The New Testament recounts a story of a Samaritan woman asking Jesus about the appropriate place to worship, Jerusalem (as it was for the Jews) or [Mount Gerizim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Gerizim) (as it was for the [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans)), to which Jesus replies:

> Woman, believe me, the hour is coming when neither on this mountain nor in Jerusalem will you worship the Father. You worship what you do not know; we worship what we know, for salvation is from the Jews. But the hour is coming, and is now here, when the true worshipers will worship the Father in spirit and in truth, for the Father is seeking such people to worship him. God is spirit, and those who worship him must worship in spirit and truth.

— John 4\:21–24[^cite_note-156]

This has been construed to mean that Jesus dispensed with physical location for worship, which was a matter rather of spirit and truth.[^cite_note-157]

Main article: [Holiest sites in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam)

See also: [Holiest sites in Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Sunni_Islam) and [Holiest sites in Shia Islam](21935937.md)

Among both [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) and [Shia](26961.md) Muslims, the entire plaza, known as the al-Aqsa Mosque, also known as Haram al-Sharif or "the Noble Sanctuary", is considered the [third holiest site in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-\:22-21] According to Islamic tradition, the plaza is the location of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad)'s [ascension to heaven from Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj), and served as the first "*[qibla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla)*", the direction Muslims turn towards when praying. As in Judaism, Muslims also associate the site with [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham), and other prophets who are also venerated in Islam.[^cite_note-Quran_2\:4,_34\:13-14-24] Muslims view the site as being one of the earliest and most noteworthy places of worship of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_concept_of_God). They preferred to use the esplanade as the heart for the Muslim quarter, since it had been abandoned by Christians, to avoid disturbing the Christian quarters of Jerusalem.[^cite_note-158] Umayyad Caliphs commissioned the construction of al-Aqsa Mosque on the site, including the shrine known as the "[Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock)".[^cite_note-Nicolle,_David_19942-4] The Dome was completed in 692 CE, making it one of the oldest extant Islamic structures in the world. The [Al-Aqsa Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibli_Mosque), sometimes known as the Qibli Mosque, rest on the far southern side of the Mount, facing [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca).

#### In early Islam

Early Islam regarded the Foundation Stone as the location of Solomon's Temple, and the first architectural initiatives on the Temple Mount sought to glorify Jerusalem by presenting Islam as a continuation of Judaism and Christianity.[^cite_note-\:18-33] Almost immediately after the [Muslim conquest of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Syria#Capturing_Jerusalem) in 638 CE, [Caliph 'Omar ibn al Khatab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar), reportedly disgusted by the filth covering the site, had it thoroughly cleaned,[^cite_note-159] and granted Jews access to the site.[^cite_note-160] According to early Quranic interpreters and what is generally accepted as Islamic tradition, in 638 CE Umar, upon entering a conquered Jerusalem, consulted with [Ka'ab al-Ahbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka'ab_al-Ahbar) – a Jewish convert to Islam who came with him from [Medina](36636.md) – as to where the best spot would be to build a mosque. Al-Ahbar suggested to him that it should be behind the Rock "... so that all of Jerusalem would be before you." Umar replied, "You correspond to Judaism!" Immediately after this conversation, Umar began to clean up the site – which was filled with trash and debris – with his cloak, and other Muslim followers imitated him until the site was clean. Umar then prayed at the spot where it was believed that Muhammad had prayed before his night journey, reciting the Quranic *sura* *[Sad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sad_\(sura\))*.[^cite_note-Mosaad-161] Thus, according to this tradition, Umar thereby reconsecrated the site as a mosque.[^cite_note-162]

Muslim interpretations of the Quran agree that the Mount is the site of the Temple originally built by [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon), [considered a prophet in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_in_Islam), that was later destroyed.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-\:9-164] After the construction, Muslims believe, the temple was used for the worship of the one God by many prophets of Islam, including Jesus.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] Other Muslim scholars have used the Torah (called *[Tawrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawrat)* in Arabic) to expand on the details of the temple.[^cite_note-168] The term *Bayt al-Maqdis* (or *Bayt al-Muqaddas*), which frequently appears as a name of Jerusalem in early Islamic sources, is a cognate of the Hebrew term *bēt ha-miqdāsh* (בית המקדש), the Temple in Jerusalem.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-\:02-171] [Mujir al-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujir_al-Din), a 15th-century Jerusalemite chronicler, mentions an earlier tradition related by al-Wasti, according which "after David built many cities and the situation of the [children of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) was improved, he wanted to construct Bayt al-Maqdis and build a dome over the rock in the place that [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam) sanctified in Aelia."[^cite_note-\:18-33]

#### Isra and Mi'raj

According to the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) was transported to a site named Al-Aqsa Mosque – "the furthest place of prayer" (*al-Masjid al-'Aqṣā*) during his [Night Journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj) (*[Isra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Isra) and Mi'raj*).[^cite_note-17th2-172] The Qur'an describes how Muhammad was taken by the miraculous steed [Buraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buraq) from the [Great Mosque of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Mecca) to al-Aqsa Mosque where he prayed.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-17th2-172][^cite_note-enc-174] After Muhammad finished his prayers, the angel [Jibril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jibril) ([Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel)) traveled with him to heaven, where he met several other [prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_in_Islam) and led them in prayer:[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177]

> Glory be to the One Who took His servant ˹Muḥammad˺ by night from the [Sacred Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_al-Haram) to the Farthest Mosque whose surroundings We have blessed, so that We may show him some of Our signs. Indeed, He alone is the All-Hearing, All-Seeing.

— [Surah Al-Isra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Isra) [17\:1](https://quran.com/17?startingVerse=1)

The Qur'an does not mention the exact location of "the furthest place of prayer", and the city of Jerusalem is not mentioned by any of [its names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Jerusalem) in the Qur'an.[^cite_note-Khatib-178][^cite_note-\:9-164] According to the [Encyclopaedia of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopaedia_of_Islam), the phrase was originally understood as a reference to a site in the heavens.[^cite_note-\:8-179] A group of Islamic scholars understood the story of Muhammad's ascension from al-Aqsa Mosque as relating to the [Jewish Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem). Another group disagreed with this identification and preferred the meaning of the term as referring to heaven.[^cite_note-Colby2008-180] [Al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Bukhari) and [Al-Tabari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Tabari), for example, are believed to have rejected the identification with Jerusalem.[^cite_note-\:8-179][^cite_note-\:10-181] Eventually, a consensus emerged around the identification of the "furthest place of prayer" with Jerusalem, and by implication the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-Colby2008-180][^cite_note-182] Later *[hadiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)* referred to Jerusalem as the site of the Al-Aqsa Mosque:[^cite_note-183]

> Narrated Jabir bin \`Abdullah:\
> &#x20;That he heard Allah's Messenger saying, "When the people of Quraish did not believe me (i.e. the story of my Night Journey), I stood up in Al-Hijr and Allah displayed Jerusalem in front of me, and I began describing it to them while I was looking at it."

— *[Sahih al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahih_al-Bukhari)* [3886](https://sunnah.com/bukhari\:3886)



A depiction of Muhammad's ascent to heaven by [Sultan Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Mohammed)

Some scholars point to the political motives of the [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_dynasty) which led to the sanctification of Jerusalem in Islam. According to the Encyclopaedia of Islam, the Night Journey was associated with Jerusalem by the Umayyads as a political means to advance the glory of Jerusalem to compete with the glory of the sanctuary in Mecca then controlled by [Abd Allah ibn al-Zubayr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_Allah_ibn_al-Zubayr).[^cite_note-\:8-179][^cite_note-184] The construction of the Dome of the Rock was interpreted by [Ya'qubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya'qubi), a 9th-century [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) historian, as an Umayyad attempt to redirect the [Hajj](23487999.md) from Mecca to Jerusalem by creating a rival to the [Ka'aba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba).[^cite_note-185]

Other academics attribute the holiness of Jerusalem to the rise and expansion of a certain type of literary genre, known as *al-Fadhail* or history of cities. The Fadhail of Jerusalem inspired Muslims, especially during the Umayyad period, to embellish the sanctity of the city beyond its status in the holy texts.[^cite_note-186] Based on the writings of the eighth century historians [Al-Waqidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Waqidi)[^cite_note-187] and [al-Azraqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azraqi), some scholars have suggested that al-Aqsa Mosque mentioned in the Qur'an is not in Jerusalem but in the village of [al-Ju'ranah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ju%CA%BDranah), 18 miles northeast of Mecca.[^cite_note-\:10-181][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

Later medieval scripts, as well as modern-day political tracts, tend to classify al-Aqsa Mosque as the third holiest site in Islam.[^cite_note-Webster-190]

#### First qibla



Al-Aqsa Mosque in 2019

The historical significance of al-Aqsa Mosque in Islam is further emphasized by the fact that Muslims turned towards al-Aqsa when they prayed for a period of 16 or 17 months after [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijra_\(Islam\)) to [Medina](36636.md) in 624; it thus became the *[qibla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla)* ("direction") that Muslims faced for prayer.[^cite_note-17th-191] Muhammad later prayed towards the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca) after receiving a revelation during a prayer session[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] in the [Masjid al-Qiblatayn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_al-Qiblatayn).[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] The *qibla* was relocated to the Kaaba where Muslims have been directed to pray ever since.[^cite_note-196]

#### Religious status

The [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) refers to al-Aqsa Mosque as the third holiest site in Islam (and calls for Arab sovereignty over it).[^cite_note-197]

## History

Main article: [Archaeological remnants of the Jerusalem Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_remnants_of_the_Jerusalem_Temple)

### Pre-Israelite

The hill is believed to have been inhabited since the [4th millennium BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_millennium_BCE). An amulet bearing the [cartouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartouche) of [Thutmose III](155730.md) (r. 1479–1425 BCE) was discovered by the [Temple Mount Sifting Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_Sifting_Project) at the site in 2012.[^cite_note-198]

### Israelite period

Main article: [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Temple)



The Immer Bulla (late 7th–early 6th century BCE), inscribed in [Paleo-Hebrew script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Hebrew_alphabet), recovered during the [Temple Mount Sifting Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_Sifting_Project) from unstratified earth removed from the compound. The patronym *Immer* corresponds to a priestly family associated with temple administration in biblical texts.

While direct archaeological excavation within the compound has not been possible, many historians and biblical archaeologists reconstruct the site as the religious center and royal acropolis of the [Kingdom of Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Judah) during the Iron Age II.[^cite_note-\:2-199] The [First Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) is believed to have once been a part of a much larger royal complex.[^cite_note-\:6-200] The Bible also mentions several other buildings constructed by Solomon at the site, including the royal palace, the "House of the Lebanon Forest", the "Hall of Pillars", the "Hall of Throne" and the "House of Pharaoh's Daughter".[^cite_note-\:20-38][^cite_note-201] Some scholars believe that, in accordance with biblical accounts, the royal and religious compound on the Temple Mount was built by Solomon during the 10th century BCE as a separate entity, which was later incorporated into the city.[^cite_note-\:2-199] Knauf argued that the Temple Mount already served as the cultic and governmental center of Jerusalem as early as in the [Late Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age).[^cite_note-202] Alternatively, Na'aman suggested that Solomon built the Temple on a much smaller scale than the one described in the Bible, which was enlarged or rebuilt during the 8th century BCE.[^cite_note-203] In 2014, [Finkelstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Finkelstein), Koch and [Lipschits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oded_Lipschits) proposed that the [tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_\(archaeology\)) of ancient Jerusalem lies beneath the modern-day compound, rather than the nearby archeological site known as the [City of David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_David_\(archaeological_site\)), as mainstream archaeology believes;[^cite_note-204] however, this proposal, known as "the mound on the Mount" theory, was rejected by other scholars of the subject.[^cite_note-205]



Remains of a wall in the northwest part of the elevated platform; [Ritmeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leen_Ritmeyer) suggested that it is the top of a remaining stone course of the western wall of the Iron Age compound.

All scholars agree that the Iron Age Temple Mount was smaller than the Herodian compound still visible today. Some scholars, such as [Kathleen Kenyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathleen_Kenyon) and [Leen Ritmeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leen_Ritmeyer), argued that the walls of the First Temple compound extended eastward as far as the [Eastern Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Wall).[^cite_note-\:2-199][^cite_note-\:6-200] Ritmeyer identifies specific courses of visible [ashlars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashlars) located to the north and south of the [Golden Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Gate_\(Jerusalem\)) as Judean Iron Age in style, dating them to the construction of this wall by [Hezekiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezekiah). More such stones are supposed to survive underground.[^cite_note-RitmeyerMount-206][^cite_note-ShanksHouse-207] Ritmeyer has also suggested that one of the steps leading to the Dome of the Rock is actually the top of a remaining stone course of the western wall of the Iron Age compound.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209]

The First Temple [was destroyed in 587/586 BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(587_BC\)) by the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) under [Nebuchadnezzar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebuchadnezzar_II).

### Persian, Hellenistic and Hasmonean periods

Main article: [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple)

Construction of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) began under [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) in around 538 BCE and was completed in 516 BCE. It was built at the original site of Solomon's Temple.[^cite_note-Schiffman-210][^cite_note-\:20-38]

Joseph Patrich and Marcos Edelcopp reconstruct a project of elevation and leveling of the outer court under High Priest [Simon II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_II_\(High_Priest\)) (identified with [Simeon the Just](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_the_Just), who is mentioned in the *[Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah)*), during his tenure from around 219 to 196 BCE. The project included an approximately square courtyard of about 197.5 × 197.5 metres, supported by retaining walls, surrounded by [stoas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoa), and enclosing a precinct defined by the soreg (which itself defined a slight smaller, 186 x 186 metres square). They connect this project to the praise of Simon in the [Book of Sirach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Sirach) for his restoration of the "House," his strengthening of the "Temple," and his laying of the foundations for an elevated courtyard enclosing the sacred precinct, from which non-Jews were prohibited, as well as his construction of a reservoir. Patrich and Edelcopp also use two documents of [Seleucid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) king [Antiochus III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_III_the_Great) as evidence for Simon's construction works. One indicated a colonnaded court surrounding the sanctuary, while the ot her concerns restrictions on non-Jewish access.[^cite_note-\:202-211]<sup>: 321, 330–334 </sup>

Building activity continued during the [Maccabean Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabean_Revolt). Following his retaking of the city in 164 BCE, [Judas Maccabeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judas_Maccabeus) is said to have fortified the precinct, constructing towers and raising the walls to defend it against the isolated Seleucid garrison at the [Acra fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acra_\(fortress\)) to the south. Around 163/62 BCE, [Lysias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysias_\(Syrian_chancellor\)) and [Antiochus V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_V_Eupator) captured the precinct and razed its new walls. Later, in 152 BCE, Judas's brother and successor [Jonathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Apphus) restored the walls and added towers.[^cite_note-\:202-211]<sup>: 335–336 </sup>

Construction at the site continued under the [Hasmonean dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_dynasty), founded by Judas and Jonathan's brother [Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Thassi), who secured [Judea's independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_Judea) and whose descendants ruled it as a sovereign state. Patrich and Edelcopp contend that Simon's son, [John Hyrcanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hyrcanus) (r. 134–104 BCE), expanded the complex to the south and east, attaining for ideological reasons the ideal area of 500 × 500 cubits (approx. 262 x 262 meters) described in [Ezekiel's vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezekiel's_Temple). This is the same size recommended by the roughly contemporary [Temple Scroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Scroll) and later retrospectively described by the Mishnah (*[Middot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middot_\(Talmud\))* 2\:1).[^cite_note-\:2022-212]<sup>: 321, 338–340 </sup> Patrich and Edelcopp argue that part of the present eastern wall follows as Hasmonean line which Herod later extended.[^cite_note-\:2022-212]<sup>: 351 </sup> They adopt the 500 x 500 cubits square identified by Ritmeyer as plausible, but reject his Iron Age/Persian period dating and attribute it instead to the Hasmonean period.[^cite_note-\:2022-212]<sup>: 351–353 </sup>

In 67 BCE [a quarrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_civil_war) broke out between [Aristobulus II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristobulus_II) and [Hyrcanus II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyrcanus_II), sons of [Alexander Jannaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Jannaeus) and [Salome Alexandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Alexandra), on the Hasmonean throne. [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) general [Pompey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey), who had been invited to intervene in the conflict, sided with Hyrcanus; Aristobulus and his followers barricaded themselves inside the Temple Mount and destroyed the bridge linking it to the city. When the Roman Army arrived in Jerusalem, Pompey ordered the moat defending the Temple Mount from the north to be filled in. To accomplish this, Pompey waited for [Shabbats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat), so the defenders would not disrupt the work. After a [three-month siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(63_BC\)), the Romans were able to topple one of the guard towers and storm the Temple Mount. Pompey himself entered the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies), but did not harm the Temple, and allowed the priests to continue their work as usual.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215]

### Herodian and early Roman periods

Main articles: [Second Temple § Herod's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple#Herod's_Temple), [Archaeological remnants of the Jerusalem Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_remnants_of_the_Jerusalem_Temple), and [Siege of Jerusalem (70 CE) § Destruction of the Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)#Destruction_of_the_Second_Temple)

Scale model of the Second Temple in first-century Jerusalem, showing the sanctuary at the center, the surrounding Temple courts enclosed by colonnades and walls, the Royal Stoa along the southern edge, and the city beyond

Scale model reconstruction of the Herodian Temple Mount complex, part of the [Holyland Model of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holyland_Model_of_Jerusalem) at the [Israel Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Museum)

Around 19 BCE, [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great) further expanded the Temple Mount and [rebuilt the temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod's_Temple). The ambitious project, which involved the employment of 10,000 workers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen200369-216] more than doubled the size of the Temple Mount to approximately 36 acres (150,000 m<sup>2</sup>). Herod leveled the area by cutting away rock on the northwest side and raising the sloping ground to the south. He achieved this by constructing huge buttress walls and vaults and filling the necessary sections with earth and rubble.[^cite_note-Negev2005p265-217] The result was the largest [temenos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temenos) in the ancient world.[^cite_note-\:0-218]

The main entrances to the Herodian Temple Mount were [two sets of gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huldah_Gates) built into the southern wall, together with four other gates reachable from the western side by stairs and bridges. Grand [stoas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoa) encircled the platform on three sides, and on its southern side stood a magnificent basilica [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) referred to as the [Royal Stoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Stoa_\(Jerusalem\)).[^cite_note-\:0-218] The Royal Stoa served as a center for the city's commercial and legal transactions, and was provided with separate access to the city below via the [Robinson's Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinson's_Arch) overpass.[^cite_note-219] The Temple itself and its courts were located on an elevated platform in the middle of the larger compound. In addition to the restoration of the Temple, its courtyards and porticoes, Herod also built the [Antonia Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonia_Fortress), which dominated the northwestern corner of the Temple Mount, and a rainwater reservoir, [Birket Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birket_Israel), in the northeast. A monumental street, today referred to as the "[Stepped Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stepped_street_\(Jerusalem\))", took pilgrims from the city's southern gate via the Tyropoeon Valley to the western side of the Temple Mount. It has been proposed in 2019 that [Pontius Pilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontius_Pilate) constructed the road during the 30s.[^cite_note-220]

### Destruction of the Temple (70 CE)

Main article: [Siege of Jerusalem (70 CE) § Destruction of the Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)#Destruction_of_the_Second_Temple)

During the early phases of the [First Jewish–Roman War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_War) (66–70 CE), the Temple Mount became a center of fighting for various Jewish factions struggling for control of the city, with different factions holding the area during the conflict. In April 70, the Roman army under [Titus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titus) reached Jerusalem and began [besieging the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)). It took the Romans four months to defeat the Temple Mount's defenders and take the site. The Romans completely destroyed the Temple and all the other structures on the platform.[^cite_note-\:7-221]



Stones from the Temple Mount complex lying on the ancient street below the [Western Wall](75225.md) at the [Jerusalem Archaeological Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Archaeological_Park)

Massive stone collapses from the upper walls were discovered laying over the Herodian street that runs along the southern part of the Western Wall.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] Excavations also recovered hundreds of architectural fragments below the southern wall, attributed on stylistic grounds to the Royal Stoa. Geological analysis found white crusts and gray discoloration on the stones consistent with soot absorption and conversion to [calcium oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium_oxide), indicating sustained burning above 800 °C (1,470 °F). One [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone) [rosette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosette_\(design\)) also preserved a veneer of bone dust fused into its surface through [vitrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitrification), though whether the bone was human or animal could not be determined.[^cite_note-224]



The [Trumpeting Place inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpeting_Place_inscription), a stone (2.43x1 m) with [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) inscription לבית התקיעה להב "To the Trumpeting Place" excavated by [Benjamin Mazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Mazar) at the southern foot of the Temple Mount is believed to be a part of the Second Temple.

Near the southern end of the Western Wall, excavators recovered the [Trumpeting Place inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpeting_Place_inscription), a stone marking the spot where a priest would sound a trumpet to announce the beginning and end of [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat). The inscription's placement corresponds to a station described by [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus), who writes that Jewish rebels built a tower above this same spot to gain height for hurling missiles at the Roman legionaries below.[^cite_note-225]

### Middle/Late Roman period

&#x20;The city of [Aelia Capitolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aelia_Capitolina) was built in 130 CE by the Roman emperor [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian) and occupied by a [Roman colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_colony) on the site of Jerusalem, which was still in ruins from the [First Jewish Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Jewish_Revolt) in 70 CE. *Aelia* came from Hadrian's *[nomen gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_naming_convention)*, *[Aelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aelia_\(gens\))*, while *Capitolina* meant that the new city was dedicated to [Jupiter Capitolinus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter_Capitolinus), to whom [a temple was built](7810.md#History) overlapping the site of the former second Jewish temple, the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-226]



Roman [centaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaur) relief (135–325 CE) reused as a floor panel in the al-Aqsa Mosque, was found during restoration work in the 1930s

Hadrian had intended the construction of the new city as a gift to the Jews, but since he had constructed a giant statue of himself in front of the Temple of Jupiter and the Temple of Jupiter had a huge statue of Jupiter inside it, there were on the Temple Mount now two enormous [graven images](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), which Jews considered idolatrous. It was also customary in [Roman rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman_religion) to sacrifice a [pig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pig) in land purification ceremonies.[^cite_note-227] After the [Third Jewish Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Jewish_Revolt), all Jews were forbidden on pain of death from entering the city or the surrounding territory around the city.[^cite_note-Lester2010-228]

In circa 318 CE, the Christian scholar [Eusebius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eusebius) wrote that, in his time, stones from the ruined Temple Mount were still being reused in pagan construction projects.[^cite_note-Koltun-Fromm2019-229] In his *Proof of the Gospel*, writes: "It is sad for the eyes to see stones from the Temple itself, and from its ancient sanctuary and holy place, used for the building of idol temples, and of theatres for the populace."[^cite_note-Ferrar1920-230] In the same work he also provided evidence for guided tours in which Christian pilgrims were brought to the [Mount of Olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_of_Olives) to look out over the ruined Temple Mount.[^cite_note-231]

### Byzantine period

Emperor [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) promoted the Christianization of Roman society, giving it precedence over pagan cults.[^cite_note-232] One consequence was that Hadrian's Temple to [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter_\(mythology\)) on the Temple Mount was demolished immediately following the [First Council of Nicea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicea) in 325 CE on orders of Constantine.[^cite_note-233]

The [Bordeaux Pilgrim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itinerarium_Burdigalense), who visited Jerusalem in 333–334, during the reign of Emperor Constantine I, wrote that "There are two statues of Hadrian, and, not far from them, a pierced stone to which the Jews come every year and anoint. They mourn and rend their garments, and then depart."[^cite_note-234] The occasion is assumed to have been [Tisha b'Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_b'Av), since decades later [Jerome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome) related that that was the only day on which Jews were permitted to enter Jerusalem.[^cite_note-Tasfrir-235]

Constantine's nephew Emperor [Julian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_the_Apostate) granted permission in the year 363 for the Jews to rebuild the Temple.[^cite_note-Tasfrir-235][^cite_note-236] In a letter attributed to Julian he wrote to the Jews that "This you ought to do, in order that, when I have successfully concluded the war in Persia, I may rebuild by my own efforts the sacred city of Jerusalem, which for so many years you have longed to see inhabited, and may bring settlers there, and, together with you, may glorify the Most High God therein."[^cite_note-Tasfrir-235] Julian saw the Jewish God as a fitting member of the pantheon of gods he believed in, and he was also a strong opponent of Christianity.[^cite_note-Tasfrir-235][^cite_note-237] Church historians wrote that the Jews began to clear away the structures and rubble on the Temple Mount but were thwarted, first by a great earthquake, and then by miracles that included fire springing from the earth.[^cite_note-PetersJulian-238] However, no contemporary Jewish sources mention this episode directly.[^cite_note-Tasfrir-235]

During his excavations in the 1930s, [Robert Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hamilton_\(archaeologist\)) uncovered portions of a multicolor mosaic floor with geometric patterns, but did not publish them.[^cite_note-Baruch13-239] The date of the mosaic is disputed: [Zachi Dvira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zachi_Dvira) considers that they are from the pre-Islamic Byzantine period, while Baruch, Reich and Sandhaus favor a much later [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad) origin on account of their similarity to a mosaic from an Umayyad palace excavated adjacent to the Temple Mount's southern wall.[^cite_note-Baruch13-239] By comparing the photographs to Hamilton's excavation report, Di Cesare determined that they belong to the second phase of mosque construction in the Umayyad period.[^cite_note-DiCesare-240] Moreover, the mosaic designs were common in Islamic, Jewish and Christian buildings from the 2nd to the 8th century.[^cite_note-DiCesare-240] Di Cesare suggested that Hamilton did not include the mosaics in his book because they were destroyed to explore beneath them.[^cite_note-DiCesare-240]

### Sassanid period

See also: [Jewish revolt against Heraclius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_revolt_against_Heraclius) and [Byzantine–Sasanian War of 602–628](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_War_of_602%E2%80%93628)

In 610, the [Sassanid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanid_Empire) drove the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) out of the Middle East, giving the Jews control of Jerusalem for the first time in centuries. The Jews in Palestine were allowed to set up a vassal state under the Sassanid Empire called the *Sassanid Jewish Commonwealth* which lasted for five years. Jewish rabbis ordered the restart of animal sacrifice for the first time since the time of Second Temple and started to reconstruct the Jewish Temple. Shortly before the Byzantines took the area back five years later in 615, the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanid_Empire) gave control to the Christian population, who tore down the partially built Jewish Temple edifice and turned it into a garbage dump,[^cite_note-241] which is what it was when the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun) [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph) [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar) took the city in 637.

### Early Muslim period



The Southwest *[qanatir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mawazin)* (arches) of the Haram al Sharif; Qubat al-Nahawiyya is also partially visible to the right.

In 637, Arabs [besieged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(637\)) and captured the city from the Byzantine Empire, which had defeated the Persian forces and their allies, and reconquered the city.
