There are no contemporary records, but many traditions, about the origin of the main Islamic buildings on the mount.[^cite_note-BahatAtlas-242][^cite_note-243] A popular account from later centuries is that the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph) [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar) was led to the place reluctantly by the Christian patriarch [Sophronius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophronius_of_Jerusalem).[^cite_note-PetersJ-244] He found it covered with rubbish, but the sacred Rock was found with the help of a converted Jew, [Ka'b al-Ahbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka'b_al-Ahbar).[^cite_note-PetersJ-244] Al-Ahbar advised Umar to build a mosque to the north of the rock, so that worshippers would face both the rock and Mecca, but instead Umar chose to build it to the south of the rock.[^cite_note-PetersJ-244] It became known as al-Aqsa Mosque. According to Muslim sources, Jews participated in the construction of the haram, laying the groundwork for both al-Aqsa and the Dome of the Rock mosques.[^cite_note-245] The first known eyewitness testimony is that of the pilgrim [Arculf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arculf) who visited about 670. According to Arculf's account as recorded by [Adomnán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adomn%C3%A1n), he saw a rectangular wooden house of prayer built over some ruins, large enough to hold 3,000 people.[^cite_note-BahatAtlas-242][^cite_note-246]

In 691, an octagonal Islamic building topped by a dome was built by the Caliph [Abd al-Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Malik_ibn_Marwan) around the rock, for a myriad of political, dynastic and religious reasons, built on local and Quranic traditions articulating the site's holiness, a process in which textual and architectural narratives reinforced one another.[^cite_note-247] The shrine became known as the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock) (قبة الصخرة, *[Qubbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qubbat) as-Sakhra*). (The dome itself was covered in gold in 1920.) In 715, the Umayyads, led by the Caliph [al-Walid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Walid_I), built al-Aqsa Mosque (المسجد الأقصى, *al-Masjid al-'Aqṣā*, lit. "Furthest Mosque"), corresponding to the Islamic belief of Muhammad's miraculous [nocturnal journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj) as recounted in the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith). The term "Noble Sanctuary" or "Haram al-Sharif", as it was called later by the [Mamluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluks) and [Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks), refers to the entirer area that surrounds that Rock.[^cite_note-248]

### Crusader and Ayyubid period



[Baldwin II of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_II_of_Jerusalem), assigning the captured [Al-Aqsa Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibli_Mosque) to [Hugues de Payens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugues_de_Payens) and Godfrey

The Crusader period began in 1099 with the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade)'s capture of Jerusalem. After the city's conquest, the Crusading order known as the [Knights Templar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Templar) was granted use of Al-Aqsa Mosque to use as their headquarters. This was probably by [Baldwin II of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_II_of_Jerusalem) and [Warmund, Patriarch of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warmund,_Patriarch_of_Jerusalem) at the [Council of Nablus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Nablus) in January 1120.[^cite_note-249] The Temple Mount had a mystique because it was above what were believed to be the ruins of the [Temple of Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple).[^cite_note-HC-250][^cite_note-251] The Crusaders therefore referred to al-Aqsa Mosque as Solomon's Temple, and it was from this location that the new Order took the name of "Poor Knights of Christ and the Temple of Solomon", or "Templar" knights.

In 1187, once he retook Jerusalem, [Saladin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin) removed all traces of Christian worship from the Temple Mount, returning the Dome of the Rock and al-Aqsa Mosque to their Muslim purposes. It remained in Muslim hands thereafter, even during the relatively short periods of Crusader rule following the [Sixth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Crusade).

### Mamluk period

There are several Mamluk buildings on and around the Haram esplanade, such as the late 15th-century [al-Ashrafiyya Madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ashrafiyya_Madrasa) and [Sabil (fountain) of Qaytbay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fountain_of_Qayt_Bay). The Mamluks also raised the level of Jerusalem's Central or Tyropoean Valley bordering the Temple Mount from the west by constructing huge substructures, on which they then built on a large scale. The Mamluk-period substructures and over-ground buildings are thus covering much of the Herodian western wall of the Temple Mount.

### Ottoman period

Following the Ottoman conquest of Palestine in 1516, the Ottoman authorities continued the policy of prohibiting non-Muslims from setting foot on the Temple Mount until the early 19th century, when non-Muslims were again permitted to visit the site.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252]



Temple Mount, photographed by [Francis Bedford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bedford_\(photographer\)), 1862

In 1867, a team from the [Royal Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Engineers), led by Lieutenant [Charles Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Warren) and financed by the [Palestine Exploration Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Exploration_Fund) (P.E.F.), discovered a series of tunnels near the Temple Mount. Warren secretly excavated some tunnels near the Temple Mount walls and was the first one to document their lower courses. Warren also conducted some small-scale excavations inside the Temple Mount, by removing rubble that blocked passages leading from the [Double Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Double_Gate) chamber.

### British Mandatory period

Main article: [1929 Palestine riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Palestine_riots)

Between 1922 and 1924, the Dome of the Rock was restored by the Islamic Higher Council.[^cite_note-Hashemite-253] The Zionist movement at the time was strongly opposed to any notion that the Temple itself might be rebuilt. Indeed, its armed wing, the [Haganah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haganah) militia, assassinated a Jewish man when his plan to blow up the Islamic sites on the Haram came to their attention in 1931.[^cite_note-254] Renovations were also conducted at the [Al-Aqsa Mosque](65759.md) from 1938–1942, following an earthquake in the summer of 1937.[^cite_note-255]

### Jordanian period



[King Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan) flying over the Temple Mount while it was under [Jordanian control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordanian_annexation_of_the_West_Bank), 1965

Jordan undertook two renovations of the Dome of the Rock, replacing the leaking, wooden inner dome with an aluminum dome in 1952, and, when the new dome leaked, carrying out a second restoration between 1959 and 1964.[^cite_note-Hashemite-253]

Neither Israeli Arabs nor Israeli Jews could visit their holy places in the Jordanian territories during this period.[^cite_note-Gilbert254-256][^cite_note-Israeli23-257]

### Israeli period



Israeli paratroopers entering the Temple Mount through the Lions Gate in 1967

On 7 June 1967, during the [Six-Day War](29328.md), Israeli forces advanced beyond the [1949 Armistice Agreement Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_Agreement_Line) into [West Bank](33209.md) territories, taking control of the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md), inclusive of the Temple Mount.

The Chief Rabbi of the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md), [Shlomo Goren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shlomo_Goren), led the soldiers in religious celebrations on the Temple Mount and at the Western Wall. The Israeli Chief Rabbinate also declared a religious holiday on the anniversary, called "[Yom Yerushalayim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Yerushalayim)" (Jerusalem Day), which became a national holiday to commemorate the [reunification of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reunification_of_Jerusalem). Many saw the capture of Jerusalem and the Temple Mount as a miraculous liberation of biblical-messianic proportions.[^cite_note-258] A few days after the war over 200,000 Jews flocked to the Western Wall in the first mass Jewish pilgrimage near the Mount since the destruction of the Temple in 70 CE. Islamic authorities did not disturb Goren when he went to pray on the Mount until, on the [Ninth Day of Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_B'Av), he brought 50 followers and introduced both a [shofar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shofar), and a portable ark to pray, an innovation which alarmed the Waqf authorities and led to a deterioration of relations between the Muslim authorities and the Israeli government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–155\]-259]

In June 1969, [an Australian set fire to the Jami'a al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_mosque_fire). On April 11, 1982, a Jew hid in the Dome of the Rock and sprayed gunfire, killing 2 Palestinians and wounding 44; in 1974, 1977 and 1983 groups led by [Yoel Lerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoel_Lerner) conspired to blow up both the Dome of the Rock and al-Aqsa. On 26 January 1984 Waqf guards detected members of B'nei Yehuda, a messianic cult of former gangsters turned mystics based in [Lifta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifta), trying to infiltrate the area to blow it up.[^cite_note-Klein-260][^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262]

On 15 January 1988, during the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada), [Israeli troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_troops) fired [rubber bullets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber_bullet) and [tear gas](1202646.md) at protesters outside the mosque, wounding 40 worshipers.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

On October 8, 1990, Israeli forces patrolling the site blocked worshippers from reaching it. A tear gas canister was set off among the female worshippers, which caused events to escalate. On 12 October 1990 Palestinian Muslims protested violently the intention of some extremist Jews to lay a cornerstone on the site for a New Temple as a prelude to the destruction of the Muslim mosques. The attempt was blocked by Israeli authorities but demonstrators were widely reported as having stoned Jews at the Western Wall.[^cite_note-Klein-260][^cite_note-265] According to Palestinian historian [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi), investigative journalism has shown this allegation to be false.[^cite_note-266] Rocks were eventually thrown, while security forces fired rounds that killed 21 people and injuring 150 more.[^cite_note-Klein-260] An Israeli inquiry found Israeli forces at fault, but it also concluded that charges could not be brought against any particular individuals.[^cite_note-267]

On 8 October 1990, [22 Palestinians were killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Temple_Mount_riots) and over 100 others injured by [Israeli Border Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Border_Police) during protests that were triggered by the announcement of the [Temple Mount Faithful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_Faithful), a group of religious Jews, that they were going to lay the cornerstone of the Third Temple.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-Ahram-269] Between 1992 and 1994, the Jordanian government undertook the unprecedented step of gilding the dome of the Dome of the Rock, covering it with 5000 gold plates, and restoring and reinforcing the structure. [Saladin's minbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minbar_of_the_al-Aqsa_Mosque) was also reconstructed. The project was paid for by [King Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan) personally, at a cost of \$8 million.[^cite_note-Hashemite-253] The Temple Mount remains, under the terms of the 1994 [Israel–Jordan peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Jordan_peace_treaty), under [Jordanian custodianship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemite_custodianship_of_Jerusalem_holy_sites).[^cite_note-Itamar-270] In December 1997, Israeli security services preempted an attempt by Jewish extremists to throw a pig's head wrapped in the pages of the Quran into the area, in order to spark a riot and embarrass the government.[^cite_note-Klein-260]

On 28 September 2000, then-opposition leader of Israel [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) and members of the [Likud Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud_Party), along with 1,000 armed guards, visited the al-Aqsa compound. The visit was seen as a provocative gesture by many Palestinians, who gathered around the site. After Sharon and the Likud Party members left, a demonstration erupted and Palestinians on the grounds of the [Haram al-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram_al-Sharif) began [throwing stones](44279911.md) and other projectiles at Israeli riot police. Police fired tear gas and rubber bullets at the crowd, injuring 24 people. The visit sparked a five-year uprising by the Palestinians, commonly referred to as the [al-Aqsa Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Intifada), though some commentators, citing subsequent speeches by Palestinian Authority officials, particularly Imad Falouji and [Yasar Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat), claim that the Intifada had been planned months in advance, as early as July upon Arafat's return from Camp David talks in the United States.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-toameh-272][^cite_note-atlantic-273] On 29 September, the Israeli government deployed 2,000 riot police to the mosque. When a group of Palestinians left the mosque after [Friday prayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumu'ah) (*Jumu'ah,*) they hurled stones at the police. The police then stormed the mosque compound, firing both live ammunition and rubber bullets at the group of Palestinians, killing four and wounding about 200.[^cite_note-274]

On 3 January 2023, Israeli Minister of National Security [Itamar Ben-Gvir](54504115.md) visited the Temple Mount in [Jerusalem](16043.md), sparking protests by [Palestinians](23267.md) and the condemnation of several [Arab countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_countries).[^cite_note-275]

## Status quo

### Under Muslim control

Jews were not allowed to visit for approximately one thousand years.[^cite_note-276]

### British Mandate

In the first ten years of British rule in Palestine, all were allowed entry to the Temple Mount/Haram al-Sharif complex. Sometimes violence broke out at the entrance between Jews and Muslims. During the [1929 Palestine riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Palestine_riots), Jews were accused of violating the status quo.[^cite_note-AmericanJewish1929-277][^cite_note-Jerusalem_one_city-278] Following the riots, the [Supreme Muslim Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Muslim_Council) and the [Jerusalem Islamic Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Islamic_Waqf) prohibited Jews from entering the site's gates. During the mandate period, Jewish leaders celebrated ancient religious practices at the Western Wall. The ban on visitors continued until 1948[^cite_note-279]

### Jordanian control (1948–1967)

Although Article VIII of the [1949 Israel–Jordan Armistice Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_Agreements) provided for the resumption of normal operations at the cultural and humanitarian institutions on [Mount Scopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Scopus), free access to religious sites, and the continued use of the [Mount of Olives Jewish Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_of_Olives_Jewish_Cemetery), these provisions were never implemented.[^cite_note-280] The city remained divided by barbed wire, walls, and a fortified no man's land.&#x20;

During the partition, Israeli Jews were barred from visiting the [Western Wall](75225.md) and the Haram al-Sharif / Temple Mount]], while Israeli Arabs were largely unable to access holy sites in the Old City. Numerous Jewish religious sites, synagogues, and cemeteries in the Jordanian-controlled sector were damaged, destroyed, or repurposed, while Muslim and Christian heritage sites in Israeli-controlled West Jerusalem also suffered damage, expropriation, or neglect.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

### Under Israeli control

A few days after the [Six-Day War](29328.md), on June 17, 1967, a meeting was held at the al-Aqsa mosque between [Moshe Dayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshe_Dayan) and Muslim religious authorities of Jerusalem reformulating the status quo.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–55\]-283] Jews were given the right to visit the Temple Mount unobstructed and free of charge if they respected Muslims' religious feelings and acted decently, but they were not allowed to pray. The Western Wall was to remain the Jewish place of prayer. 'Religious sovereignty' was to remain with the Muslims while 'overall sovereignty' became Israeli.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–155\]-259] The Muslims objected to Dayan's offer, as they completely rejected the Israeli conquest of Jerusalem and the Temple Mount. Some Jews, led by [Shlomo Goren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shlomo_Goren), then the military chief rabbi, had objected as well, claiming the decision handed over the complex to the Muslims, since the Western Wall's holiness is derived from the Mount and symbolizes exile, while praying on the Mount symbolizes freedom and the return of the Jewish people to their homeland.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–55\]-283] The President of the High Court of Justice, [Aharon Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aharon_Barak), in response to an appeal in 1976 against police interference with an individual's putative right to prayer on the site, expressed the view that, while Jews had a right to prayer there, it was not absolute but subject to the public interest and the rights of other groups. Israel's courts have considered the issue as one beyond their remit, and, given the delicacy of the matter, under political jurisdiction.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–55\]-283] Barak wrote:

> The basic principle is that every Jew has the right to enter the Temple Mount, to pray there, and to have communion with his maker. This is part of the religious freedom of worship, it is part of the freedom of expression. However, as with every human right, it is not absolute, but a relative right... Indeed, in a case where there is near certainty that injury may be caused to the public interest if a person's rights of religious worship and freedom of expression would be realized, it is possible to limit the rights of the person in order to uphold the public interest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–155\]-259]

Police continued to forbid Jews to pray on the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–55\]-283] Subsequently, several prime ministers also made attempts to change the status quo but failed. In October 1986, an agreement between the [Temple Mount Faithful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_Faithful), the Supreme Muslim Council and police, which would allow short visits in small groups, was exercised once and never repeated, after 2,000 Muslims armed with stones and bottles attacked the group and stoned worshipers at the Western Wall. During the 1990s, additional attempts were made for Jewish prayer on the Temple Mount, which were stopped by Israeli police.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonen2003\[httpsarchiveorgdetailscontestedholines00rivkpage149_149–55\]-283]

Until 2000, non-Muslim visitors could enter the Dome of the Rock, al-Aqsa Mosque and the Islamic Museum by getting a ticket from the *Waqf*. This procedure ended when the [Second Intifada](71717.md) erupted. Fifteen years later, negotiation between Israel and Jordan might result in reopening of those sites once again.[^cite_note-284]

In the 2010s, fear arose among Palestinians that Israel planned to change the status quo and permit Jewish prayers or that al-Aqsa mosque might be damaged or destroyed by Israel. Al-Aqsa was used as a base for attacks on visitors and the police from which stones, firebombs and fireworks were thrown. The Israeli police had never entered al-Aqsa Mosque until November 5, 2014, when dialog with the leaders of the *Waqf* and the rioters failed. This resulted in imposing strict limitations on entry of visitors to the Temple Mount. Israeli leadership repeatedly stated that the status quo would not change.[^cite_note-Shragai-285] According to then Jerusalem police commissioner Yohanan Danino, the place is at the center of a "holy war" and "anyone who wants to change the status quo on the Temple Mount should not be allowed up there", citing an "extreme right-wing agenda to change the status quo on the Temple Mount"; Hamas and Islamic Jihad continued to erroneously assert that the Israeli government planned to destroy the al-Aqsa Mosque, resulting in chronic terrorist attacks and rioting.[^cite_note-286]

There have been several changes to the status quo:

1. Jewish visits are often prevented or considerably restricted.

2. Jews and other non-Islamic visitors can only visit from Sunday to Thursday, for four hours each day.

3. Visits inside the mosques are not allowed.

4. Jews with religious appearance must visit in groups monitored by *Waqf* guards and policemen.

   [^cite_note-Shragai-285]

Many Palestinians believe the status quo is threatened since right-wing Israelis have been challenging it with more force and frequency, asserting a religious right to pray there. Until Israel banned them, members of [Murabitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murabitat), a group of women, cried 'Allah Akbar' at groups of Jewish visitors to remind them the Temple Mount was still in Muslim hands.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] In October 2021, a Jewish man, Aryeh Lippo, who was banned by Israeli police from the Temple Mount for fifteen days after being caught quietly praying, had his ban overturned by an Israeli court on the grounds that his behavior had not violated police instructions.[^cite_note-289] Hamas called the ruling "a clear declaration of war".[^cite_note-290] A higher Israel court quickly reversed the lower court's ruling.[^cite_note-291]

## Management and access

See also: [Temple Mount entry restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_entry_restrictions)



A security gate guarding the entrance to the site

An [Islamic Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Waqf) has managed the Temple Mount continuously since the Muslim reconquest of the [Latin Kingdom of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Jerusalem) in 1187. On June 7, 1967, soon after Israel had taken control of the area during the [Six-Day War](29328.md), Prime Minister [Levi Eshkol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi_Eshkol) assured that "no harm whatsoever shall come to the places sacred to all religions". Together with the extension of Israeli jurisdiction and administration over east Jerusalem, the [Knesset](74656.md) passed the Preservation of the Holy Places Law,[^cite_note-292] ensuring protection of the Holy Places against desecration, as well as freedom of access thereto.[^cite_note-293] The site remains within the area controlled by the [State of Israel](9282173.md), with administration of the site remaining in the hands of the Jerusalem Islamic Waqf.

Although freedom of access was enshrined in the law, as a security measure, the Israeli government now enforces a ban on non-Muslim prayer on the site. Non-Muslims who are observed praying on the site are subject to expulsion by the police.[^cite_note-294] At various times, when there is fear of Arab rioting upon the mount resulting in [throwing stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_stone-throwing) from above towards the Western Wall Plaza, Israel has prevented Muslim men under 45 from praying in the compound, citing these concerns.[^cite_note-295] Sometimes such restrictions have coincided with [Friday prayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumuah) during the Islamic holy month of [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan).[^cite_note-296] Normally, West Bank Palestinians are allowed access to Jerusalem only during Islamic holidays, with access usually restricted to men over 35 and women of any age eligible for permits to enter the city.[^cite_note-297] Palestinian residents of Jerusalem, which because of Israel's annexation of Jerusalem, hold Israeli permanent residency cards, and Israeli Arabs, are permitted unrestricted access to the Temple Mount. The [Mughrabi Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount) is the only entrance to the Temple Mount accessible to non-Muslims.[^cite_note-Mughrabi-298][^cite_note-MughrabiAlJazeera-299][^cite_note-300]

## Jewish attitudes towards entering the site



Sign in [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew) and English outside the Temple Mount stating that "According to the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), it is forbidden for any person to enter the area of the Temple Mount due to its sacredness"

Due to religious restrictions on entering the most sacred areas of the Temple Mount (see following section), the [Western Wall](75225.md), a retaining wall for the Temple Mount and remnant of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) structure, is considered by some rabbinical authorities to be the holiest accessible site for Jews to pray. A 2013 [Knesset](74656.md) committee hearing considered allowing Jews to pray at the site, amidst heated debate. [Arab-Israeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab-Israeli) MPs were ejected for disrupting the hearing, after shouting at the chairman, calling her a "pyromaniac". Religious Affairs Minister [Eli Ben-Dahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Ben-Dahan) of [Jewish Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Home) said his ministry was seeking legal ways to enable Jews to pray at the site.[^cite_note-301]

### Jewish religious law concerning entry to the site

Main article: [Temple Warning inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Warning_inscription)

During Temple times, entry to the Mount was limited by a complex set of [purity laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumah). Persons suffering from [corpse uncleanness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpse_uncleanness) were not allowed to enter the inner court.[^cite_note-302] Non-Jews were also prohibited from entering the inner court of the Temple.[^cite_note-303] A hewn stone measuring 60 cm × 90 cm (24 in × 35 in) and engraved with [Greek uncials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncial_script) was discovered in 1871 near a court on the Temple Mount in Jerusalem in which it outlined this prohibition:

> ΜΗΟΕΝΑΑΛΛΟΓΕΝΗΕΙΣΠΟ\
> &#x20;ΡΕΥΕΣΟΑΙΕΝΤΟΣΤΟΥΠΕ\
> &#x20;ΡΙΤΟΙΕΡΟΝΤΡΥΦΑΚΤΟΥΚΑΙ\
> &#x20;ΠΕΡΙΒΟΛΟΥΟΣΔΑΝΛΗ\
> &#x20;ΦΘΗΕΑΥΤΩΙΑΙΤΙΟΣΕΣ\
> &#x20;ΤΑΙΔΙΑΤΟΕΞΑΚΟΛΟΥ\
> &#x20;ΘΕΙΝΘΑΝΑΤΟΝ

Translation: "Let no foreigner enter within the parapet and the partition which surrounds the Temple precincts. Anyone caught \[violating] will be held accountable for his ensuing death." Today, the stone is preserved in [Istanbul's Museum of Antiquities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Antiquities,_Istanbul).

[Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides) wrote that it was only permitted to enter the site to fulfill a religious precept. After the destruction of the Temple there was discussion as to whether the site, bereft of the Temple, still maintained its holiness. Jewish codifiers accepted the opinion of Maimonides who ruled that the holiness of the Temple sanctified the site for eternity and consequently the restrictions on entry to the site remain in force.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252] While secular Jews ascend freely, the question of whether ascending is permitted is a matter of some debate among religious authorities, with a majority holding that it is permitted to ascend to the Temple Mount, but not to step on the site of the inner courtyards of the ancient Temple.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252] The question then becomes whether the site can be ascertained accurately.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252]

There is debate over whether reports that [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides) himself ascended the Mount are reliable.[^cite_note-304] One such report claims that he did so on Thursday, October 21, 1165, during the Crusader period. Some early scholars however, claim that entry onto certain areas of the Mount is permitted. It appears that [Radbaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_ben_Solomon_ibn_Abi_Zimra) also entered the Mount and advised others how to do this. He permits entry from all the gates into the 135 x 135 [cubits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubits) of the Women's Courtyard in the east, since the biblical prohibition only applies to the 187 x 135 cubits of the Temple in the west.[^cite_note-305] There are also Christian and Islamic sources which indicate that Jews visited the site,[^cite_note-306] but these visits may have been made under duress.[^cite_note-307]

Those persons who suffered a [nocturnal emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturnal_emission) and who have not yet [immersed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual_washing_in_Judaism#Full-body_immersion_\(Tevilah\)) themselves in a [ritual bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikveh) were permitted to enter the Court of the Israelites, but were prohibited from entering the Court of the Levites and the Court of the Priests until they had immersed.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309] Entrance into the areas of the other two courts was strictly forbidden to those who had not immersed themselves and who had been defiled by [corpse uncleanness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpse_uncleanness) and who had not yet been purified. Since the latter state of impurity is pervasive, and since there is an inability to be purified from its effects, many rabbis forbid entrance into the Temple Mount altogether as a safeguard.[^cite_note-310]

### Opinions of contemporary rabbis concerning entry to the site



[Haredi Jews](53835.md) visiting the Temple Mount during [Passover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover)

A few hours after the Temple Mount came under Israeli control during the Six-Day War, a message from the [Chief Rabbis of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbinate_of_Israel), [Isser Yehuda Unterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isser_Yehuda_Unterman) and [Yitzhak Nissim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Nissim) was broadcast, warning that Jews were not permitted to enter the site.[^cite_note-Inbari22-311] This warning was reiterated by the Council of the Chief Rabbinate a few days later, which issued an explanation written by Rabbi Bezalel Jolti (Zolti) that "Since the sanctity of the site has never ended, it is forbidden to enter the Temple Mount until the Temple is built."[^cite_note-Inbari22-311] The signatures of more than 300 prominent rabbis were later obtained.[^cite_note-CohenTemple-312]

A major critic of the decision of the Chief Rabbinate was Rabbi Shlomo Goren, the chief rabbi of the IDF.[^cite_note-Inbari22-311] According to General [Uzi Narkiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzi_Narkiss), who led the Israeli force that conquered the Temple Mount, Goren proposed to him that the Dome of the Rock be immediately blown up.[^cite_note-CohenTemple-312] After Narkiss refused, Goren unsuccessfully petitioned the government to close off the Mount to Jews and non-Jews alike.[^cite_note-CohenTemple-312] Later he established his office on the Mount and conducted a series of demonstrations on the Mount in support of the right of Jewish men to enter there.[^cite_note-Inbari22-311] His behavior displeased the government, which restricted his public actions, censored his writings, and in August prevented him from attending the annual Oral Law Conference at which the question of access to the Mount was debated.[^cite_note-Hassner-313] Although there was considerable opposition, the conference consensus was to confirm the ban on entry to Jews.[^cite_note-Hassner-313] The ruling said "We have been warned, since time immemorial \[lit. 'for generations and generations'], against entering the entire area of the Temple Mount and have indeed avoided doing so."[^cite_note-CohenTemple-312][^cite_note-Hassner-313] According to Ron Hassner, the ruling "brilliantly" solved the government's problem of avoiding ethnic conflict, since those Jews who most respected rabbinical authority were those most likely to clash with Muslims on the Mount.[^cite_note-Hassner-313]

Rabbinical consensus in the post-1967 period, held that it is forbidden for Jews to enter any part of the Temple Mount,[^cite_note-314] and in January 2005, a declaration was signed confirming the 1967 decision.[^cite_note-315]

Most [Haredi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haredi) rabbis are of the opinion that the Mount is off limits to Jews and non-Jews alike.[^cite_note-316] Their opinions against entering the Temple Mount are based on the current political climate surrounding the Mount,[^cite_note-317] along with the potential danger of entering the hallowed area of the Temple courtyard and the impossibility of fulfilling the ritual requirement of cleansing oneself with the ashes of a [red heifer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Heifer).[^cite_note-rabbinaterole-318][^cite_note-aharbay-319] The boundaries of the areas which are completely forbidden, while having large portions in common, are delineated differently by various rabbinic authorities.

However, there is a growing body of [Modern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox) and [national religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionism) rabbis who encourage visits to certain parts of the Mount, which they believe are permitted according to most medieval rabbinical authorities.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252] These rabbis include: Shlomo Goren (former Ashkenazi Chief Rabbi of Israel); Chaim David Halevi (former Chief Rabbi of Tel Aviv and Yafo); [Dov Lior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dov_Lior) (Rabbi of [Kiryat Arba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiryat_Arba)); [Yosef Elboim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yosef_Elboim); [Yisrael Ariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yisrael_Ariel); [She'ar Yashuv Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/She'ar_Yashuv_Cohen) (Chief Rabbi of [Haifa](55606.md)); [Yuval Sherlo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuval_Sherlo) ([rosh yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_yeshiva) of the [hesder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesder) yeshiva of [Petah Tikva](202411.md)); [Meir Kahane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meir_Kahane). One of them, Shlomo Goren, held that it is possible that Jews are even allowed to enter the heart of the Dome of the Rock in time of war, according to Jewish Law of Conquest.[^cite_note-haaretz-320] These authorities demand an attitude of veneration on the part of Jews ascending the Temple Mount, [ablution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual_washing_in_Judaism) in a [mikveh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikveh) prior to the ascent, and the wearing of non-leather shoes.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252] Some rabbinic authorities are now of the opinion that it is imperative for Jews to ascend in order to halt the ongoing process of [Islamization of the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_the_Temple_Mount). Maimonides, perhaps the greatest codifier of Jewish Law, wrote in *Laws of the Chosen House ch 7 Law 15* "One may bring a dead body in to the (lower sanctified areas of the) Temple Mount and there is no need to say that the ritually impure (from the dead) may enter there, because the dead body itself can enter". One who is ritually impure through direct or in-direct contact of the dead cannot walk in the higher sanctified areas. For those who are visibly Jewish, they have no choice, but to follow a peripheral route[^cite_note-321] as it has become unofficially part of the status quo on the Mount. Many of these recent opinions rely on archaeological evidence.[^cite_note-MeyerMessner-252]

In December 2013, the two Chief Rabbis of Israel, [David Lau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lau) and [Yitzhak Yosef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Yosef), reiterated the ban on Jews entering the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-DLYY-322] They wrote, "In light of \[those] neglecting \[this ruling], we once again warn that nothing has changed and this strict prohibition remains in effect for the entire area \[of the Temple Mount]".[^cite_note-DLYY-322] In November 2014, the Sephardic chief rabbi Yitzhak Yosef reiterated the point of view held by many rabbinic authorities that Jews should not visit the Mount.[^cite_note-Itamar-270]

On the occasion of an upsurge in Palestinian knifing attacks on Israelis, associated with fears that Israel was changing the status quo on the Mount, the Haredi newspaper Mishpacha ran a notification in Arabic asking, 'their cousins', Palestinians, to stop trying to murder members of their congregation, since they were vehemently opposed to ascending the Mount and consider such visits proscribed by Jewish law.[^cite_note-323]

## Features

### Courtyard

The large courtyard (*[sahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahn)*)[^cite_note-Prawer-22] can host more than 400,000 worshippers, making it one of the [largest mosques in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_mosques).[^cite_note-NG-20]

#### Upper platform

The upper platform surrounds the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock), beneath which lies the [Well of Souls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Well_of_Souls), originally accessible only by a narrow hole in the [Sakhrah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhrah), the foundation stone on which the Dome of the Rock site and after which it is named, until the [Crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) dug a new entrance to the cave from the south.[^cite_note-324] The platform is accessible via eight staircases, each of which is topped by a free-standing [arcade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcade_\(architecture\)) known in Arabic as the [*qanatir* or *mawazin*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mawazin). The arcades were erected in different periods from the 10th to 15th centuries.[^cite_note-\:21-325]

There is also a smaller domed building on the upper platform, to the east of the Dome of the Rock, known as the [Dome of the Chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Chain) (*Qubbat al-Sisila* in Arabic).[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] Its exact origin and purpose is uncertain but historical sources indicate it was built under the reign of Abd al-Malik, the same Umayyad caliph who built the Dome of the Rock.[^cite_note-328] Two other small domes stand to the northwest of the Dome of the Rock. The Dome of the Ascension (*Qubbat al-Miraj* in Arabic) has an inscription with a date corresponding to 1201 CE.[^cite_note-\:21-325][^cite_note-\:23-329] It may have been a former Crusader structure, possibly a [baptistery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptistery), that was repurposed at this time,[^cite_note-\:23-329] or it may be a structure that was built after [Saladin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin)'s capture of the city and reused some Crusader-era materials, including its columns.[^cite_note-330] Per its name, this dome commemorates the spot where, according to some, Muhammad ascended to heaven.[^cite_note-331] The Dome of the Spirits or Dome of the Winds (*Qubbat al-Arwah* in Arabic) stands a little further north and is dated to the 16th century.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-\:21-325]



Southern edge of the upper platform, with view of the Summer Pulpit (left) and the southern *qanatir* behind it

In the southwest corner of the upper platform is a quadrangular structure which includes a portion topped by another dome. It is known as the Dome of Literature (*Qubba Nahwiyya* in Arabic) and dated to 1208.[^cite_note-\:21-325] Standing further east, close to one of the southern entrance arcades, is a stone *[minbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minbar)* known as the "Summer Pulpit" or Minbar of Burhan al-Din, used for open-air prayers. It appears to be an older [ciborium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciborium_\(architecture\)) from the Crusader period, as attested by its sculptural decoration, which was then reused under the [Ayyubids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty). Sometime after 1345, a Mamluk judge named Burhan al-Din (d. 1388) restored it and added a stone staircase, giving it its present form.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

#### Lower platform



The *al-Kas* ablution fountain for Muslim worshippers on the southern portion of the lower platform

The lower platform – which constitutes most of the surface of the Temple Mount – has at its southern end al-Aqsa Mosque, which takes up most of the width of the Mount. Gardens take up the eastern and most of the northern side of the platform; the far north of the platform houses an Islamic school.[^cite_note-335]

The lower platform also houses an [ablution fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebil_\(fountain\)) (known as *al-Kas*), originally supplied with water via a [long narrow aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Pools#History) leading from the so-called *[Solomon's Pools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Pools)* near [Bethlehem](4312.md), but now supplied from Jerusalem's water mains.

There are several [cisterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisterns) beneath the lower platform, designed to collect rainwater as a water supply. These have various forms and structures, seemingly built in different periods, ranging from vaulted chambers built in the gap between the bedrock and the platform, to chambers cut into the bedrock itself. Of these, the most notable are (numbering traditionally follows Wilson's scheme[^cite_note-336]):

- Cistern 1 (located under the northern side of the upper platform). There is a speculation that it had a function connected with the altar of the Second Temple (and possibly of the earlier Temple),

  [^cite_note-Kaufman-337]

  &#x20;or with the *[bronze sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molten_Sea)*.

- Cistern 5 (located under the southeastern corner of the upper platform) – a long and narrow chamber, with a strange anti-clockwise curved section at its northwestern corner and containing within it a doorway currently blocked by earth. The cistern's position and design is such that there has been speculation it had a function connected with the altar of the Second Temple (and possibly of the earlier Temple), or with the *bronze sea*. [Charles Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Warren) thought that the *altar of burnt offerings* was located at the northwestern end.

  [^cite_note-Patrich-338]

- Cistern 8 (located just north of the al-Aqsa Mosque) – known as the *Great Sea*, a large rock hewn cavern, the roof supported by pillars carved from the rock; the chamber is particularly cave-like and atmospheric,

  [^cite_note-339]

  &#x20;and its maximum water capacity is several hundred thousand gallons.

- Cistern 9 (located just south of cistern 8, and directly under the al-Aqsa Mosque) – known as the *Well of the Leaf* due to its leaf-shaped plan, is also rock hewn.

- Cistern 11 (located east of cistern 9) – a set of vaulted rooms forming a plan shaped like the letter E. Probably the largest cistern, it has the potential to house over 700,000 gallons of water.

- Cistern 16/17 (located at the centre of the far northern end of the Temple Mount). Despite the currently narrow entrances, this cistern (17 and 16 are the same cistern) is a large, vaulted chamber, which Warren described as looking like the inside of the cathedral at [Cordoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain) (which was previously a mosque). Warren believed that it was almost certainly built for some other purpose and was only adapted into a cistern at a later date; he suggested that it might have been part of a general vault supporting the northern side of the platform, in which case substantially more of the chamber exists than is used for a cistern.

### Gates

Main article: [Gates of the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount)



The eastern set of Hulda gates



[Robinson's Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinson's_Arch), situated on the southwestern flank, once supported a staircase that led to the Mount.

**Sealed gates**

The retaining walls of the platform contain several gateways, all now blocked. In the eastern wall is [the Golden Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Gate_\(Jerusalem\)), through which legend states the [Jewish Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Messiah) would enter Jerusalem. On the southern face are the [Hulda Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulda_Gates) – the *triple gate* (which has three arches) and the *double gate* (which has two arches and is partly obscured by a Crusader building); these were the entrance and exit (respectively) to the Temple Mount from [Ophel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophel) (the oldest part of Jerusalem), and the main access to the Mount for ordinary Jews. In the western face, near the southern corner, is the Barclay's Gate – only half visible due to a building (the "house of Abu Sa'ud") on the northern side. Also in the western face, hidden by later construction but visible via the recent [Western Wall Tunnels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Wall_Tunnels), and only rediscovered by Warren, is [Warren's Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren's_Gate); the function of these western gates is obscure, but many Jews view Warren's Gate as particularly holy, due to its location due west of the Dome of the Rock. The current location of the Dome of the Rock is considered one of the possible locations where the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies) was placed; numerous alternative opinions exist, based on study and calculations, such as those of Tuvia Sagiv.

Warren was able to investigate the inside of these gates. Warren's Gate and the Golden Gate simply head toward the centre of the Mount, giving access to the surface by steps.[^cite_note-340] Barclay's Gate is similar, but abruptly turns south as it does so; the reason for this is unknown. The double and triple gates (the *Huldah Gates*) are more substantial; heading into the Mount for some distance they each finally have steps rising to the surface just north of al-Aqsa Mosque.[^cite_note-341] The passageway for each is vaulted, and has two aisles (in the case of the triple gate, a third aisle exists for a brief distance beyond the gate); the eastern aisle of the double gates and western aisle of the triple gates reach the surface, the other aisles terminating some way before the steps. Warren believed that one aisle of each original passage was extended when al-Aqsa Mosque blocked the original surface exits.

In the process of investigating Cistern 10, Warren discovered tunnels that lay *under* the Triple Gate passageway.[^cite_note-342] These passages lead in erratic directions, some leading beyond the southern edge of the Temple Mount (they are at a depth below the base of the walls); their purpose is unknown – as is whether they predate the Temple Mount – a situation not helped by the fact that apart from Warren's expedition no one else is known to have visited them.

Altogether, there are six major sealed gates and a postern, listed here counterclockwise, dating from either the Roman/Herodian, Byzantine, or Early Muslim periods:

- Bab al-Jana'iz/al-Buraq (Gate of the Funerals/of al-Buraq); eastern wall; a hardly noticeable [postern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postern), or maybe an improvised gate, a short distance south of the Golden Gate

- Golden Gate (Bab al-Zahabi); eastern wall (northern third), a double gate:

Bab al-Rahma (Door of Mercy) is the southern opening,

Bab al-Tauba (Door of Repentance) is the northern opening

- Warren's Gate; western wall, now only visible from the Western Wall Tunnel

- Bab an-Nabi (Gate of the Prophet) or Barclay's Gate; western wall, visible from al-Buraq Mosque inside the Haram, and from the Western Wall plaza (women's section) and the adjacent building (the so-called house of Abu Sa'ud)

- Double Gate (Bab al-Thulathe; possibly one of the Huldah Gates); southern wall, underneath al-Aqsa Mosque

- Triple Gate; southern wall, outside Solomon's Stables/Marwani Mosque

- Single Gate; southern wall, outside Solomon's Stables/Marwani Mosque

**Open gates of the Haram**

Main article: [Gates of the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount)

There are now eleven open gates offering access to the Muslim Haram al-Sharif.

- Bab al-Asbat (Gate of the Tribes); north-east corner

- Bab al-Hitta/Huttah (Gate of Remission, Pardon, or Absolution); northern wall

- Bab al-Atim/'Atm/Attim (Gate of Darkness); northern wall

- Bab al-Ghawanima (Gate of Bani Ghanim); north-west corner

- Bab al-Majlis / an-Nazir/Nadhir (Council Gate / Inspector's Gate); western wall (northern third)

- Bab al-Hadid (Iron Gate); western wall (central part)

- Bab al-Qattanin (Gate of the Cotton Merchants); western wall (central part)

- Bab al-Matarah/Mathara (Ablution Gate); western wall (central part)

Two twin gates follow south of the Ablution Gate, the Tranquility Gate and the Gate of the Chain:

- Bab as-Salam / al-Sakina (Tranquility Gate / Gate of the Dwelling), the northern one of the two; western wall (central part)

- Bab as-Silsileh (Gate of the Chain), the southern one of the two; western wall (central part)

- Bab al-Magharbeh/Maghariba (Moroccans' Gate/Gate of the Moors); western wall (southern third); the only entrance for non-Muslims

A twelfth gate still open during Ottoman rule is now closed to the public:

- Bab as-Sarai (Gate of the Seraglio); a small gate to the former residence of the Pasha of Jerusalem; western wall, northern part (between the Bani Ghanim and Council gates).

### Solomon's Stables/Marwani Mosque

East of and joined to the triple gate passageway is a large vaulted area, supporting the southeastern corner of the Temple Mount platform – which is substantially above the bedrock at this point – the vaulted chambers here are popularly referred to as [Solomon's Stables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Stables).[^cite_note-343] They were used as stables by the Crusaders, but were built by Herod the Great – along with the platform they were built to support.

### Northern and western porticos

The complex is bordered on the south and east by the outer walls of the Old City of Jerusalem. On the north and west it is bordered by two long porticos (*[riwaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riwaq_\(arcade\))*), built during the Mamluk period.[^cite_note-344] A number of other structures were also built along these areas, mainly also from the Mamluk period. On the north side, they include the Isardiyya Madrasa, built before 1345, and the Almalikiyya Madrasa, dated to 1340.[^cite_note-\:212-345] On the west side, they include the Ashrafiyya Madrasa, built by Sultan Qaytbay between 1480 and 1482,[^cite_note-\:03-346] and the adjacent [Uthmaniyya Madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Uthmaniyya_Madrasa_\(Jerusalem\)), dated to 1437.[^cite_note-347] The Sabil of Qaytbay, contemporary with the Ashrafiyya Madrasa, also stands nearby.[^cite_note-\:03-346]

### Minarets

Main article: [Minarets of the Al-Aqsa Mosque compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minarets_of_the_Al-Aqsa_Mosque_compound)

The existing four minarets include three along the western perimeter of the esplanade and one along the northern wall. The earliest dated minaret was constructed on the northwest corner of the Temple Mount in 1298, with three other minarets added over the course of the 14th century.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]

## Archaeology, site alterations

Main article: [Excavations at the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excavations_at_the_Temple_Mount)

See also: [Temple Denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Denial), [Islamization of the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_the_Temple_Mount), and [Judaization of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaization_of_Jerusalem)

Due to the extreme political sensitivity of the site, no real archaeological excavations have ever been conducted on the Temple Mount itself. Protests commonly occur whenever archaeologists conduct projects near the Mount. This sensitivity has not, however, protected both Jewish and Muslim works from accusations of destroying archeological evidence on a number of occasions.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] Aside from visual observation of surface features, most other archaeological knowledge of the site comes from the 19th-century survey carried out by [Charles Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_William_Wilson) and [Charles Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Warren) and others. Since the Waqf is granted almost full autonomy on the Islamic holy sites, Israeli archaeologists have been prevented from inspecting the area, and are restricted to conducting excavations around the Temple Mount.



[Southern Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Wall) of Temple Mount, southwestern corner

After the Six-Day War of 1967, Israeli archeologists began a series of excavations near the site at the [southern wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_wall) that uncovered finds from the Second Temple period through Roman, [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad) and Crusader times.[^cite_note-353] Israeli archaeological digs at the southwestern corner of Temple Mount discovered traces of four Muslim palaces built under the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), though the remains have not been well preserved but instead had a museum built upon them. The former UN envoy to Jerusalem, [Raymond M. Lemaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_M._Lemaire), criticised "the construction of a metallic pergola in the middle of the courtyard of one of the Umayyad palaces, which disfigures the site." Upon visiting Jerusalem in September 1999, medieval art historian [Léon Pressouyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Pressouyre) noted that the palaces had lost their archaeological features due to neglect, "for in the guise of highlighting the remains of previous periods \[the Israeli authorities] trivialise the Umayyad palaces, major monuments in the area".[^cite_note-354]

Over the period 1970–1988, a number of tunnels were excavated in the vicinity, including one that passed to the west of the Mount and became known as the [Western Wall Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Wall_Tunnel), which was opened to the public in 1996.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] The same year the Waqf began construction of a new mosque in the structures known since Crusader times as [Solomon's Stables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Stables). Many Israelis regarded this as a radical change of the status quo, which should not have been undertaken without first consulting the Israeli government. The project was done without attention to the possibility of disturbing historically significant archaeological material, with stone and ancient artifacts treated without regard to their preservation.[^cite_note-357]

Israeli organizations such as the [Committee to Prevent the Destruction of Antiquities on the Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Prevention_of_Destruction_of_Antiquities_on_the_Temple_Mount) argue that Palestinians are deliberately removing significant amounts of archaeological evidence about the Jewish past of the site and claim to have found significant artifacts in the fill removed by bulldozers and trucks from the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-\:5-358][^cite_note-359] Since the late 1990s, the [Temple Mount Sifting Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_Sifting_Project) has been reclaiming earth from similar illegal excavations on the mount that had been dumped in the nearby Kidron Valley that had yielded important finds, including Iron Age figurines, an 8th or 7th centuries BCE clay sealing inscribed in Hebrew, [Persian period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_\(Persian_province\)) [YHD coins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehud_coinage), Herodian [opus sectile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opus_sectile) tiles, Byzantine [tesserae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tessera), and arrowheads, mostly from the Crusader period.[^cite_note-\:5-358][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362]



[Gabriel Barkay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Barkay) presents [Moshe Ya'alon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshe_Ya'alon) with the reconstructions of the [opus sectile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opus_sectile) floors of the Herodian period plaza

In late 2002, a bulge of about 700 mm (28 in) was reported in the southern retaining wall part of the Temple Mount. A Jordanian team of engineers recommended replacing or resetting most of the stones in the affected area.[^cite_note-363] In February 2004, the eastern wall of the Mount was damaged by an earthquake. The damage threatened to topple sections of the wall into the area known as Solomon's Stables.[^cite_note-364] A few days later, a portion of retaining wall, supporting the earthen ramp that led from the Western Wall plaza to the [Gate of the Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mugrabi_Gate) on the Temple Mount, collapsed.[^cite_note-365] In 2007 the [Israel Antiquities Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Antiquities_Authority) started construction of a temporary wooden pedestrian pathway to replace the [Mugrabi Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_of_the_Temple_Mount) ramp after a landslide in 2005 made it unsafe and in danger of collapse.[^cite_note-366] The works sparked condemnation from Arab leaders.[^cite_note-367]

In July 2007 the [Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf) which administers the Mount began digging a 400-metre-long (1,300 ft), 1.5-metre-deep (4.9 ft) [trench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trench)[^cite_note-AS1-368] from the northern side of the Temple Mount compound to the [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock)[^cite_note-H1-369] in order to replace 40-year-old[^cite_note-G1-370] electric cables in the area. Israeli archaeologists accused the waqf of a deliberate act of cultural vandalism.[^cite_note-H1-369] Accusations of vandalism at the site resurfaced in 2018 and again in 2022.[^cite_note-\:5-358][^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]

## Noteworthy events

February 2004

*Partially collapsed [Mughrabi-Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughrabi-Bridge):* An 800-year-old wall holding back part of the hill jutting out from the [Western Wall](75225.md) leading up to the Mughrabi Gate partially collapsed. Authorities believed a recent earthquake may have been responsible.

[^cite_note-373]

[^cite_note-374]

March 2005

*Allah inscription:* The word "[Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah)", in approximately a one-foot-tall (0.30 m) Arabic script, was found newly carved into the ancient stones, an act viewed by Jews as vandalism. The carving was attributed to a team of [Jordanian](7515964.md) engineers and Palestinian laborers in charge of strengthening that section of the wall. The discovery caused outrage among Israeli archaeologists and many Jews were angered by the inscription at Judaism's holiest site.

[^cite_note-375]

October 2006

*Synagogue proposal:* [Uri Ariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uri_Ariel), a member of the Knesset from the [National Union party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_\(Israel\)) (a right-wing opposition party) ascended to the mount,

[^cite_note-376]

&#x20;and said that he is preparing a plan where a synagogue will be built on the mount. His proposed [synagogue](48559.md) would not be built instead of the mosques but in a separate area in accordance with rulings of 'prominent rabbis.' He said he believed that this will be correcting a historical injustice and that it is an opportunity for the Muslim world to prove that it is tolerant to all faiths.

[^cite_note-377]

*Minaret proposal:* Plans are mooted to build a new minaret on the mount, the first of its kind for 600 years.

[^cite_note-378]

&#x20;King [Abdullah II of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_II_of_Jordan) announced a competition to design a fifth minaret for the walls of the Temple Mount complex. He said it would "reflect the Islamic significance and sanctity of the mosque". The scheme, estimated to cost \$300,000, is for a seven-sided tower – after the seven-pointed Hashemite star – and at 42 metres (138 ft), it would be 3.5 metres (11 ft) taller than the next-largest minaret. The minaret would be constructed on the eastern wall of the Temple Mount near [the Golden Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Gate_\(Jerusalem\)).

February 2007

*[Mugrabi Gate ramp reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excavations_at_the_Temple_Mount#Mughrabi_Gate_ramp_replaced_by_a_bridge_\(2007-\)):* Repairs to an earthen ramp leading to the [Mugrabi Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mugrabi_Gate) sparked Arab protests.

May 2007

*Right-wing Jews ascend the Mount:* A group of right-wing Religious Zionist rabbis entered the Temple Mount.

[^cite_note-379]

&#x20;This elicited widespread criticism from other religious Jews and from secular Israelis, accusing the rabbis of provoking the Arabs. An editorial in the newspaper *[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz)* accused the rabbis of 'knowingly and irresponsibly bringing a burning torch closer to the most flammable hill in the Middle East,' and noted that rabbinical consensus in both the Haredi and the Religious Zionist worlds forbids Jews from entering the Temple Mount.

[^cite_note-380]

&#x20;On May 16, Rabbi [Avraham Shapira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avraham_Shapira), former Ashkenazi Chief Rabbi of Israel and [rosh yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_yeshiva) of the [Mercaz HaRav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercaz_HaRav) [yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshiva), reiterated his opinion that it is forbidden for Jews to enter the Temple Mount.

[^cite_note-381]

&#x20;The Litvish Haredi newspaper *[Yated Ne'eman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yated_Ne'eman_\(Israel\))*, which is controlled by leading Litvish Haredi rabbis including Rabbi [Yosef Shalom Eliashiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yosef_Shalom_Eliashiv) and Rabbi [Nissim Karelitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nissim_Karelitz), accused the rabbis of transgressing a decree punishable by 'death through the hands of heaven.'

[^cite_note-aharbay-319]

July 2007

*[Temple Mount cable replacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Mount_cable_replacement_controversy)*: The [Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf) began digging a ditch from the northern side of the Temple Mount compound to the Dome of the Rock as a prelude to infrastructure work in the area. Although the dig was approved by the police, it generated protests from archaeologists.

October 2009

*Clashes*: Palestinian protesters gathered at the site after rumours that an extreme Israeli group would harm the site, which the Israeli government denied.

[^cite_note-382]

&#x20;Israeli police assembled at the Temple Mount complex to disperse Palestinian protesters who were throwing stones at them. The police used [stun grenades](662807.md) on the protesters, of which 15 were later arrested, including the Palestinian President's adviser on Jerusalem affairs.

[^cite_note-383]

[^cite_note-384]

&#x20;Eighteen Palestinians and 3 police officers were injured.

[^cite_note-385]

July 2010

*A public opinion poll* in Israel showed that 49% of Israelis want the Temple to be rebuilt, with 27% saying the government should make active steps towards such reconstruction. The poll was conducted by channel 99, the government-owned Knesset channel, in advance of the ninth day of the Hebrew month of Av, on which Jews commemorate the destruction of both the first and second Temples, which stood at this site.

[^cite_note-386]

Knesset Member [Danny Danon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Danon) visited the Temple Mount in accordance with rabbinical views of Jewish Law on the ninth of the Hebrew Month of Av. The Knesset member condemned the conditions imposed by Muslims upon religious Jews at the site and vowed to work to improve conditions.

[^cite_note-387]

July 2017

*[Temple Mount shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Temple_Mount_shooting)*: Three men from the Israeli-Arab city of Umm al-Fahm opened fire on two Israeli [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel) policemen at the Lions' Gate.

[^cite_note-388]

&#x20;Gun attacks have been unusual at the Temple Mount in recent decades.

[^cite_note-389]

Following the July 14 attack, the site was shut down, and reopened on July 16 with metal detector-equipped checkpoints, spurring calls for protests by Muslim leaders associated with the site.

[^cite_note-390]

April 2022

[*Al-Aqsa Mosque clashes*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Al-Aqsa_clashes): On 15 April 2022, clashes erupted between Palestinians and Israeli Security Forces on the Temple Mount. The clashes began when Palestinians threw stones, firecrackers, and other heavy objects at Israeli police officers. The policemen responded with various riot control measures.

[^cite_note-\:3-391]

[^cite_note-392]

[^cite_note-393]

&#x20;Some Palestinians then barricaded themselves inside al-Aqsa Mosque and continued throwing stones at the policemen.

[^cite_note-\:3-391]

[^cite_note-\:4-394]

&#x20;In response, police raided the mosque, arresting those who had barricaded themselves inside. Some damage was done to the mosque's structure.

[^cite_note-\:3-391]

[^cite_note-auto2-395]

[^cite_note-AlArabiya1-396]

April 2023

[*Al-Aqsa Mosque clashes*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Al-Aqsa_clashes)

**December 2025**

Archaeologists in Jerusalem uncovered a 1,300-year-old lead pendant bearing a seven-branched menorah near the southwestern corner of the Temple Mount, during digs in the Davidson Archaeological Park.[^cite_note-\:11-397][^cite_note-\:14-398]

The object is almost pure lead with menorahs on both sides, and is only the second known pendant of its kind, the other held in Baltimore.[^cite_note-\:11-397][^cite_note-\:24-399]

It dates to the Late Byzantine period, when Jews were formally banned from Jerusalem, yet its presence suggests some still visited or maintained ties to the city.[^cite_note-\:11-397][^cite_note-\:14-398]

Researchers think it served as a personal amulet symbolizing Jewish identity, not simple jewelry, and its discovery at the foot of the Temple Mount reinforces the enduring Jewish historical and spiritual connection to the site.[^cite_note-\:24-399][^cite_note-\:14-398]

The holy house has most commonly assumed to be located on the same spot as the Moslem holy structure known as the Dome of the Rock. This assumption has been held for centuries for the following reasons: The rock out-cropping under the Dome of the Rock is the main natural feature within the Haram enclosure; the Dome of the Rock is centrally located within the esplanade, and, at 2,440 feet above sea level, the Dome of the Rock is one of the highests point within the area.

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;["Temple Mount"](https://www.britannica.com/place/Temple-Mount). *Britannica*. 2024-05-07. Retrieved 2024-05-27. Temple Mount, site of the Temple of Jerusalem, which was destroyed by the Romans on the 9th/10th of Av in 70 CE (see Tisha be-Av). It consists of a raised platform that, since the 7th century, has been home to the Islamic holy sites of the Dome of the Rock and Al-Aqsa Mosque. The lower section of the compound's Western Wall, which dates to the 2nd century BCE, is the only remnant of the retaining wall that surrounded the Temple. It has served as a site of pilgrimage for Jews since the Temple's destruction.

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;["Old City of Jerusalem"](https://www.britannica.com/place/Old-City-of-Jerusalem). *Britannica*. 2024-04-23. Retrieved 2024-05-27. The Old City is dominated by the raised platform of the Temple Mount – known in Hebrew as Har Ha-Bayit, the site of the First and Second Temples, and known to Islam as Al-Ḥaram al-Sharīf ("The Noble Sanctuary"), a Muslim holy place containing the Dome of the Rock, Al-Aqsa Mosque, and other structures.

Al 'Aqsa is the second oldest mosque in Islam after the Kaaba in Mecca and is third in holiness after the mosques in Mecca and Medina. It holds up to 400,000 worshippers at one time.

It is known to Muslims as al-Haram al-Sharif (the Noble Sanctuary) and to Jews and Christians as the Har ha-Bayit or Temple Mount. The whole area of the Noble Sanctuary is considered by Muslims to be the al-Aqsa Mosque, and the entire precinct is inviolable according to Islamic law. It is considered specifically part of the waqf (endowment) land that had included the Western Wall (Wailing Wall), property of an Algerian family, and more generally a waqf of all of Islam. When viewed as a complex of buildings, the al-Aqsa Mosque is dominated and bounded by two major structures: the al-Aqsa Mosque building on the east and the Dome of the Rock (or the Mosque of Omar) on the west. The Dome of the Rock is the oldest holy building in Islam. ["Jerusalem holy site clashes fuel fears of return to war"](https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-61154373). *BBC News*. 2022-04-22. Whole site also considered by Muslims as Al Aqsa Mosque. UNESCO World Heritage Centre (2022-04-04). Retrieved 2022-05-29. …the historic Gates and windows of the Qibli Mosque inside Al-Aqsa Mosque/ Al-Haram Al-Sharif, which is a Muslim holy site of worship and an integral part of a World Heritage Site. The whole enclosure of the Haram is called by Moslem writers Masjid el Aksa, 'praying-place of the Aksa,' from this mosque." [Yitzhak Reiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Reiter): "This article deals with the employment of religious symbols for national identities and national narratives by using the sacred compound in Jerusalem (The Temple Mount/al-Aqsa) as a case study. The narrative of The Holy Land involves three concentric circles, each encompassing the other, with each side having its own names for each circle. These are: Palestine/Eretz Israel (i.e., the Land of Israel); Jerusalem/al-Quds and finally The Temple Mount/al-Aqsa compound...Within the struggle over public awareness of Jerusalem's importance, one particular site is at the eye of the storm – the Temple Mount and its Western Wall – the Jewish Kotel – or, in Muslim terminology, the al-Aqsa compound (alternatively: al-Haram al-Sharif) including the al-Buraq Wall... "Al-Aqsa" for the Palestinian-Arab-Muslim side is not merely a mosque mentioned in the Quran within the context of the Prophet Muhammad's miraculous Night Journey to al-Aqsa which, according to tradition, concluded with his ascension to heaven (and prayer with all of the prophets and the Jewish and Christian religious figures who preceded him); rather, it also constitutes a unique symbol of identity, one around which various political objectives may be formulated, plans of action drawn up and masses mobilized for their realization", ["Narratives of Jerusalem and its Sacred Compound"](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/259734239_Narratives_of_Jerusalem_and_its_Sacred_Compound), *Israel Studies* 18(2): 115–32 (July 2013). Annika Björkdahl and Susanne Buckley-Zistel: "The site is known in Arabic as Haram al-Sharif – the Noble Sanctuary – and colloquially as the Haram or the al-Aqsa compound; while in Hebrew, it is called Har HaBeit – the Temple Mount." Annika Björkdahl; Susanne Buckley-Zistel (2016). [*Spatialising Peace and Conflict: Mapping the Production of Places, Sites and Scales of Violence*](https://books.google.com/books?id=YrztCwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA243). Palgrave Macmillan UK. pp. 243–. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-137-55048-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-137-55048-4). [Mahdi Abdul Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_Abdul_Hadi): "Al-Aqsa Mosque, also referred to as Al-Haram Ash-Sharif (the Noble Sanctuary), comprises the entire area within the compound walls (a total area of 144,000 m2) – including all the mosques, prayer rooms, buildings, platforms and open courtyards located above or under the grounds – and exceeds 200 historical monuments pertaining to various Islamic eras. According to Islamic creed and jurisprudence, all these buildings and courtyards enjoy the same degree of sacredness since they are built on Al-Aqsa's holy grounds. This sacredness is not exclusive to the physical structures allocated for prayer, like the Dome of the Rock or Al-Qibly Mosque (the mosque with the large silver dome)"[Mahdi Abdul Hadi](http://www.tika.gov.tr/upload/2016/INGILIZCE%20SITE%20ESERLER/TANITIM%20BRO%C5%9E%C3%9CRLER%C4%B0/PDF/Haram-Ash-sharief-Final-En_2013.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200216194529/https://www.tika.gov.tr/upload/2016/INGILIZCE%20SITE%20ESERLER/TANITIM%20BRO%c5%9e%c3%9cRLER%c4%b0/PDF/Haram-Ash-sharief-Final-En_2013.pdf) 2020-02-16 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine) [Palestinian Academic Society for the Study of International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Academic_Society_for_the_Study_of_International_Affairs); Tim Marshall: "Many people believe that the mosque depicted is called the Al-Aqsa; however, a visit to one of Palestine's most eminent intellectuals, Mahdi F. Abdul Hadi, clarified the issue. Hadi is chairman of the Palestinian Academic Society for the Study of International Affairs, based in East Jerusalem. His offices are a treasure trove of old photographs, documents, and symbols. He was kind enough to spend several hours with me. He spread out maps of Jerusalem's Old City on a huge desk and homed in on the Al-Aqsa compound, which sits above the Western Wall. "The mosque in the Al-Aqsa \[Brigades] flag is the Dome of the Rock. Everyone takes it for granted that it is the Al-Aqsa mosque, but no, the whole compound is Al-Aqsa, and on it are two mosques, the Qibla mosque and the Dome of the Rock, and on the flags of both Al-Aqsa Brigades and the Qassam Brigades, it is the Dome of the Rock shown," he said. One should bear in mind that since the emergence of nationalism in the Arab world, important schools have insisted on separation of religion and state. In addition, a degree of tension exists between al-Aqsa's two aspects, as a national symbol uniting Palestinian Muslims and Christians, and al-Aqsa as an exclusively Muslim symbol. In other words, the intentions of Palestinians united under the banner of al-Aqsa are not all the same… For the Palestinians, al-Aqsa is a singular focal point of self-respect and religious destiny. This heightens their commitment to the site, without connection to their religious affiliation (Muslim or Christian) or level of religious belief and observance.

[^cite_note-:18-33]: &#x20;Reiter, Yitzhak (2017). *Contested Holy places in Israel/Palestine: Sharing and Conflict Resolution*. Routledge. pp. 21–23. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-138-24349-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-138-24349-1). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [960842983](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/960842983). The HS is also the third holiest site in Islam. Early Islam identified the location of the Holy Rock (known as the Foundation Stone among Jews) with the Temple of Solomon. The Dome of the Rock, built by the Caliph 'Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan at the end of the seventh century CE, was aimed to glorify the place with the understanding of Islam as a continuation of Judaism (and Christianity). Muslim writers related to the site with respect to its sacred continuity. For example, the fifteenth-century Arab historian of Jerusalem Mujir al-Din quotes an early tradition narrated by al-Wasti stating, "After David built many cities and the situation of the children of Israel was improved, he wanted to construct Bayt al-Maqdis \[Jerusalem] and build a dome over the rock in the place that Allah sanctified in Aelia \[the Roman Byzantine name of Jerusalem]". In another place, he writes, "Suleiman (Solomon) built Masjid Bayt al-Maqdis by the order of his father Da'ud (David)." However, during the twentieth century, against the backdrop of the struggle between the Zionist and the Palestinian-Arab national movements, a new Arab-Muslim trend of denying Jewish attachment to the Temple Mount arose. On the Jewish side, meanwhile, some nationalists and academics also belittled the importance to Muslims of the sacred site in particular and of Jerusalem in general, highlighting the fact that Jerusalem's name never appears in the Qur'an and that the city never served as an Arab political center.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliav200850–51-36]: &#x20;[Eliav 2008](#CITEREFEliav2008), pp. 50–51: "The pair of words *Temple Mount* also debuted in the works of the prophets. The *copyright* for this name is reserved to the prophet Micah, who incorporated it into his famous admonitory prophecy: *Therefore shall Zion for your sake be plowed as a field, and Jerusalem shall become heaps, and the mountain of the house as the high places of the forest* (Micah 3\:12). It is quite doubtful, however, that the book of Micah preserved a concrete name that was actually used in the day-to-day lexicon of the prophet's generation. A close-reading of this passage shows that the phrase *Mountain of the House* is a literary variation of a longer term, the *mountain of the House of the Lord* (three words in Hebrew), which appears in verse 4\:1. The author places the complete term in the middle and *plays* with its constituent parts (both pieces come out to two words in Hebrew) in the previous and subsequent verses (3\:12; 4\:2). In verse 4\:1 the name Lord is deleted, leaving the term *Mount of the House,* or Temple Mount. This, then, is not a case of terms taken from the vocabulary of daily life but rather variations characteristic of the common literary diction used by the prophets. Furthermore, nearly one thousand years will pass from the alleged time of Micah until the specific term *Temple Mount* reappears in the Mishnah. In the interim, the term *Temple Mount* is not used in even one of the numerous existing sources, except in works quoting and using the entire phrase from Micah. This is conclusive evidence that the name *Temple Mount* was not used in earlier periods, even though the image of a mountain as a place for a temple was both known and probably, at least to some degree, widespread."
